A Resolution of the Poisson Problem for Elastic Plates
Francesca Da Lio, Francesco Palmurella*, Tristan Rivière*

Abstract
The Poisson problem consists in finding an immersed surface Σ ⊂ ℝ m \Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m} minimising Germain’s elastic energy (known as Willmore energy in geometry) with prescribed boundary, boundary Gauss map and area. This problem represents a non-linear model for the equilibrium state of thin, clamped elastic plates originating from the work of S. Germain and S.D. Poisson in the early XIX century. We present a solution to this problem in the case of boundary data of class C 1 , 1 C^{1,1} and when the boundary curve is simple and closed. The minimum is realised by an immersed disk, possibly with a finite number of branch points in its interior, which is of class C 1 , α C^{1,\alpha} up to the boundary for some 0 < α < 1 0<\alpha<1 , and whose Gauss map extends to a map of class C 0 , α C^{0,\alpha} up to the boundary.

Keywords
:
Non-linear elasticity theory,
Elastic Plates,
Willmore surfaces,
Plateau problem,
Conformal differential geometry,
Liouville-type equation,
Higher-order ellitpic PDEs,
Integrability by compensation,
Neumann problem.

1
Introduction

Given a curve
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
m\geq 3
, consisting possibly of several connected components,
a unit normal (
m-2
)-vector field
\vec{n}_{0}
along
\Gamma
,
and a given positive number
A>0
,
the
Poisson problem for
\Gamma
,
\vec{n}_{0}
and
A
consists in finding
an oriented, immersed surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}

which is a minimiser of the energy

\displaystyle W(\Sigma)=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{H}_{\Sigma}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Sigma},

(1.1)

among all surfaces having boundary
\Gamma
,
Gauss map along
\Gamma
equal to
\vec{n}_{0}

and area
A
.
Here,
\vec{H}_{\Sigma}
and
\mathrm{d}vol_{\Sigma}
denote, respectively,
the mean curvature vector of
\Sigma

and the area element of
\Sigma
induced by the immersion
of
\Sigma
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
The name is after S.D. Poisson’s memoir,
[
56
]
,
who considered equilibrium states of thin, clamped elastic plates,
and found that critical points of the energy
(
1.1
)
(see Fig.
1
).

Figure 1:
An excerpt from Poisson’s 1814 (pub. 1816) memoir where he considers
the Lagrangian nowadays known as Willmore functional.

The hypothesis that the elastic energy density be proportional to the mean curvature
is due to S. Germain’s seminal research on elastic plates
(later collected in the monograph
[
32
]
),
who in turn built on earlier one-dimensional models concerning the vibration of elastic beams investigated
by Euler and Jacques Bernoulli.
Variants of such hypothesis are still in use
nowadays for the study of equilibria of elastic plates,
albeit often in a “linearised” version
where (
1.1
) is replaced by the simpler biharmonic energy
of a graph.
We refer for instance to
[
43
,
65
,
31
]
for more on
this subject,
and to
[
13
,
17
,
64
]
for a historical perspective on the
development of the subject.
The energy (
1.1
) is nowadays known
as
Willmore energy
after T.J. Willmore
[
68
]
and R. Bryant

[
12
]
who re-introduced it in a modern and geometric perspective.
Finally, we remark that the Poisson problem may be seen
as a generalisation of the Plateau’s problem
(see for instance
[
63
,
14
,
23
,
24
]
),
since, for certain special choices of the field
\vec{n}_{0}
and of
A
,
there will be minimal surfaces
\Sigma
(i.e. satisfying
\vec{H}_{\Sigma}=0
)
bounding
\Gamma
which are then absolute minimisers for (
1.1
).

When the topology of the surface
\Sigma
is fixed, the Poisson problem can be reformulated
in a more tractable way that we now describe.
If
g_{\Sigma}
and
\vec{\II}_{\Sigma}
denote the induced metric and the second fundamental
form of
\Sigma
and, for every
x\in\Sigma
,
|\vec{\II}_{\Sigma}(x)|_{g_{\Sigma}}

denotes the norm of
\vec{\II}_{\Sigma}(x)
on

(T_{x}\Sigma,g_{\Sigma}(x))
1
1

1

Geometrically, we have
|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}(x)|^{2}=k_{1}(x)^{2}+k_{2}(x)^{2}
,
where
k_{1}(x)
and
k_{2}(x)
are the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
at
x
.
,
the
total curvature energy
of
\Sigma
is defined as

\displaystyle E(\Sigma)=\int_{\Sigma}|\vec{\II}_{\Sigma}|^{2}_{g_{\Sigma}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Sigma}.

(1.2)

Since there holds

\displaystyle|\vec{\II}_{\Sigma}(x)|^{2}_{g_{\Sigma}}=4|\vec{H}_{\Sigma}(x)|^{2}-2K_{\Sigma}(x),

(1.3)

where
K_{\Sigma}
denotes the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
,
we have that

\displaystyle 4W(\Sigma)-E(\Sigma)=2\int_{\Sigma}K_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Sigma}.

By virtue of the Gauss-Bonnet theorem (see for instance
[
1
, Chapter 6]
)
there holds

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}K_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Sigma}=2\pi\chi(\Sigma)-\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g},

(1.4)

where
\chi(\Sigma)
denotes the Euler-Poincaré characteristic of
\Sigma

and
\int_{\partial\Sigma}k_{g}
is the integral (or sum of integrals when
\partial\Sigma
consists of
multiple connected components) of the geodesic curvature of
\partial\Sigma

as a positively oriented curve in
\Sigma
.
Hence, if the topology of
\Sigma
is fixed, the difference between
the functionals
4W
and
E
is a null Lagrangian,
consequently the Willmore and the total curvature energy are equivalent from
the variational point of view for the treatment of the Poisson problem.
The advantage of working with the total curvature energy is that it has a better
coercivity property and that it controls the number of branch points
2
2

2

We recall that, roughly speaking, a branch point is a point
so that the immersion
\Sigma\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
degenerates.
A classical example is the following: in
\mathbb{R}^{4}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}
consider the map

\Phi(z)=(z^{2},z^{3})
,
for
z\in B_{1}(0)
.
Then
\Sigma=\Phi(B_{1}(0))
is a surface which is branched at the origin.
Branch points play an important role in the study of minimal and
Willmore surfaces. We refer for instance to the monographs
[
14
,
59
]
.

with
their multiplicities (see for instance
[
50
]
or
[
59
]
),
so the variational problem will be well-posed with
E
.

We observe that there could be differences between minimizers of the two Lagrangians
W(\Sigma)
and
E(\Sigma)
in the case of the presence of interior branch points, since the identity (
1.4
) does not hold anymore.

We present in this paper a solution to the Poisson problem,
in the case where
\Gamma
is a connected, simple, closed curve
and
\Sigma
is (the image of) a parametrised, possibly branched, immersed disk

\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
(here we denote
B_{1}(0)=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|z|<1\}
).
Let us define the class of “admissible” data
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

for which we can solve the problem.

Definition 1.1
.

A triple
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

curve
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, a unit-normal
(m-2)
-vector field

\vec{n}_{0}
and a real number
A>0
is called
admissible for the Poisson problem

if
\Gamma
and
\vec{n}_{0}
are of class
C^{1,1}
,

\Gamma
is simple and closed, and if there is at least one map

\Phi\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
so that

(i)

\Phi
is conformal, i.e. it satisfies

\displaystyle|\partial_{1}\Phi|=|\partial_{2}\Phi|\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\partial_{1}\Phi,\partial_{2}\Phi\rangle=0\quad\text{a.e. in }B_{1}(0),

and it is a
C^{1}
-immersion
up to the boundary
away from a finite number of
interior
branch points,

(ii)

the Gauss map
\vec{n}_{\Phi}
of
\Phi
is of class
W^{1,2}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x<+\infty,

(iii)

if
\gamma:[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

is a chosen arc-length parametrisation of
\Gamma
,
there exist a homeomorphism
\sigma_{\Phi}:S^{1}\to[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim

so that, for every
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)=S^{1}

there holds

\displaystyle\Phi(x)=\gamma(\sigma_{\Phi}(x))\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{n}_{\Phi}(x)=\vec{n}_{0}(\gamma(\sigma_{\Phi}(x))),

(iv)

there holds

\displaystyle\Area(\Phi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla\Phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=A.

Note that, for a given triple
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
it is not so obvious
to determine directly whether is it admissible of not.
However, an elementary application of the
h-principle

(see
[
28
,
36
]
) allows us, for any given
\Gamma
and

\vec{n}_{0}
as in definition
1.1
,
to prove the existence of some
A_{0}>0
such that, for every
A\geq A_{0}

the triple
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
is admissible.
Such a construction is presented in appendix
A.1

(lemma
A.1
).
In particular when
m=3
, if one requires the map
\Phi
as in the definition

1.1
not to have any branch points,
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

need to satisfy a topological constraint, namely, if
\mathbf{t}
denotes the
tangent vector of
\Gamma
, the map

\displaystyle x\mapsto(\mathbf{t}\times\vec{n}_{0},\mathbf{t},\vec{n}_{0})(x),\quad x\in S^{1},

has to define a non-nullhomotopic loop in the space of
special orthogonal matrices
SO(3)
.

The first main result of this paper is the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
be an admissible triple for the Poisson problem.
Then, there exists a map
\Phi_{\infty}:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
verifying this data which
is a
C^{1}(B_{1}(0)\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\})
conformal immersion, where
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\}
is the finite set (possibly empty) of branched points in
B_{1}(0)
,
minimising the total curvature energy
E
in this class.

The second main result is the following.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
be an admissible triple for the Poisson problem.
Every minimising map
\Phi_{\infty}
as in theorem
1.2

satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle\divop\left(\nabla\vec{H}_{\Phi_{\infty}}-3\pi_{\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}}}(\nabla\vec{H}_{\Phi_{\infty}})+\star(\nabla^{\perp}\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}}\wedge\vec{H}_{\Phi_{\infty}})+c\nabla\Phi_{\infty}\right)=0\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}),

(1.5)

where
\pi_{n_{\Phi_{\infty}}}
is the orthogonal projection
onto the normal bundle of
\Phi_{\infty}
,

\nabla^{\perp}\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}}=(-\partial_{2}\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}},\partial_{1}\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}})

and
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, there exists
0<\alpha<1
so that
\Phi_{\infty}
is of class

C^{1,\alpha}
up to the boundary and
\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}}

extends to a map of class
C^{0,\alpha}
up to the boundary.

We recall that any map
\Phi_{\infty}
as in theorem
1.3

is smooth in
B_{1}(0)
away from its branch points
(this follows from an adaptation of the results in
[
57
]
,
similarly as done in
[
49
,
50
]
)
and, since the equation (
1.5
) is satisfied
through the branch points, from the study of the singularities of Willmore
surfaces (see
[
57
,
7
]
and
[
41
,
42
]
)
it follows that, for every
0<\beta<1
,

\Phi_{\infty}
is of class
C^{2,\beta}
at the branch points
and its Gauss map
\vec{n}_{\Phi_{\infty}}
extends to a map
of class
C^{1,\beta}
at the branch points.

Remark 1.4
.

Notice that the assumptions
\Gamma,\vec{n_{0}}\in W^{2,\infty}
are far from being optimal. We expect that the conclusions of theorems
1.2

1.3
to hold under weaker assumptions on the admissible boundary data.

Let us put our results in a broader context.
Nitsche
[
53
]
discussed various boundary conditions
for the Willmore and related type of functionals, and proved existence
and uniqueness results for a class of such problems,
also considering a volume constraint, when the surfaces are graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the boundary data
are sufficiently small in
C^{4,\alpha}
-norm.
Recently Deckelnick-Grunau-Rögers
[
22
]
also consider
the minimisation over graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the Willmore functional
(also plus a constant times integral of the Gauss curvature) subject to
various boundary conditions and deduced compactness results in the

L^{1}
-topology, and from this, also a lower-semicontinuity for a suitably
defined relaxation of the Willmore functional.
A considerable series of results
(we refer to
[
27
,
22
,
19
,
6
,
20
,
5
,
21
,
18
]

and the references therein) is available when considering boundary value problems
for the Willmore functional under the hypothesis that the surfaces in consideration
are surfaces of revolution around an axis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(hence the boundary consist of two circles).
Schätzle
[
61
]
,
by working on the sphere
S^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, has proved the existence,
for arbitrary smooth boundary data
\Gamma
and
\vec{n}_{0}
and without area constraint,
of a branched immersion, smooth away from the finitely many branch points,
satisfying the strong form of the Willmore equation in
S^{m}
, namely

\displaystyle\Delta^{S^{m}}_{g}\vec{H}_{\Phi,S^{m}}+Q(\mathring{\II}_{\Phi,S^{m}})\vec{H}_{\Phi,S^{m}}=0,

(1.6)

away from the branch points
(we refer to the paper in question or to
the monograph by Kuwert and the same author in
[
48
]

for more detail on this equation).
However this equation has no meaning at the branch points and
it is not proved that, starting from arbitrary data
\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0}

in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, projecting them stereographically into
S^{m}
,
and then considering the projected-back Willmore surface obtained in
S^{m}

(recall that the Willmore equation is conformally invariant), one gets
a surface which is “Poisson-minimal”, i.e. that it solves the Poisson problem
or also whether it passes through
\infty
or not.

Figure 2:
Euler-Lagrange Equation for Willmore Energy derived by Poisson.

Concerning the equation (
1.6
), we mention that it already appeared in Poisson’s memoirs for special cases of graphs, (see Fig.
2
). This is actually
striking since at the time Lagrange’s theory was not so developed.

Alexakis-Mazzeo
[
3
]
consider smooth, properly embedded and complete Willmore
surfaces in the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{3}
and relate the regularity
of their asymptotic boundary with the smallness of a suitable version of the Willmore energy.
In a recent paper Alessandroni-Kuwert

[
2
]
, considering a free-boundary problem for the Willmore functional, have proved the existence (and non-uniqueness) of smooth Willmore disk-type surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

with prescribed but small value of the area whose boundary lays on
the boundary of a smooth, bounded domain.

At this point we would like to mention some interesting questions and open problems related to the
the Poisson problem, some of which are the aim of future investigation.
Beside our expectations regarding the boundary regularity
(see remark
1.4
) one may for instance consider:

•

existence and properties of non-minimising i.e. saddle-type Willmore surfaces
having prescribed boundary data and area
(a partial answer is in
[
61
]
described above);

•

the solution to the Poisson problem in the case of a boundary curve consisting
of multiple connected components
(in the spirit of its Plateau-counterpart as done in the classical work by Meeks-Yau

[
47
]
) or among manifold with a non-trivial topology
(an important tool for this sould be the work by Bauer-Kuwert
[
4
]
),
or both;

•

an investigation on a version of the Poisson problem where
no area bound is prescribed;

•

free-boundary versions of the Poisson problem in the case of surfaces with
arbitrary prescribed area and boundary laying in some submanifold of

\mathbb{R}^{m}
such as the sphere (as done in the forementioned work
[
2
]
but
with no restriction on the prescribed value of the area).

Let us discuss some central issues involved in our proofs.
Our approach to the Poisson problem
is in the framework of the so-called weak immersions with

L^{2}
-bounded second fundamental form, developed by the third author
in
[
57
,
60
]
(see also the monographs
[
58
,
59
]
).
As it has been discovered in
[
57
]
, a central role
in the analysis of Willmore surfaces is played by peculiar estimates that are valid
for PDEs involving Jacobian non-linearities.
In a nutshell, one considers the following Dirichlet problem:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\partial_{1}a\,\partial_{2}b-\partial_{2}a\,\partial_{1}b&&\text{in}\,B_{1}(0),\\
u&=0&&\text{on}\,\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.7)

where
a,b\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
.
It is well-known since Wente’s work (see
[
66
,
67
]
and
[
10
]
)
that, for every
a,b
, the solution
u
to
(
1.7
) is
C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
and satisfies

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\|\nabla b\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}.

(1.8)

for some constant
C>0
independent of
u,a
and
b

(see appendix
A.2
).
The estimate (
1.8
) is a priori not trivial since, being
on a first glance
\Delta u
only in
L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
, the standard Calderón-Zygmund
estimates do not apply.
Such sharp estimates allow for a successful treatment of many issues regarding both
the compactness and the regularity of Willmore surfaces (we refer to
[
59
]
).
In the case of the Poisson problem, however, it turns out that
the central role is played by a corresponding Neumann problem, namely

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\partial_{1}a\,\partial_{2}b-\partial_{2}a\,\partial_{1}b&&\text{in}\,B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=\partial_{\tau}a\,b&&\text{on}\,\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.9)

for
a,b\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
,
where
\partial_{\tau}
denotes the tangential derivative along
\partial B_{1}(0)
.
The first and second author has recently shown in
[
16
]

(see also
[
39
]
) that, although any solution of (
1.9
)
is naturally in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
(we refer again to the discussion in appendix
A.2
),
there are explicit counter-examples to the
L^{\infty}
-estimate
if no further assumptions are made on
a
and
b

(one could even find counter-examples to the
W^{1,2}
-estimate if
a homogeneous boundary condition is chosen, see
[
16
]
).
We will prove that
through suitable a-priori estimates on the general Neumann problem
similar to those appearing in
[
11
,
15
]
and
a careful analysis on the boundary behaviour of the solution
of (
1.9
), a sufficient condition to achieve such crucial estimate
will be on the trace of
a,b
to be in
W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))
, for
some
p>1
.

The regularity
up to the boundary
of a minimising map of (
1.2
) in theorem
1.3
follows from a boundary
\varepsilon
-regularity Lemma which is in turn obtained by suitable comparison arguments.
It is expected such an
\varepsilon
-regularity Lemma up to the boundary to hold for general critical points of the Energy (
1.2
) (an interior
\varepsilon
-regularity for
such critical points has been already proved by the third author in
[
57
]
).

We conclude by introducing some basic notation and geometric notions that will be used throughout the paper.

1.1
Basic notation

Beside the common notation,
in this paper we denote:

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}

\displaystyle=\{(x^{1},x^{2}):x^{2}>0\}

\displaystyle\text{upper half-space},

\displaystyle B_{r}^{+}(0)

\displaystyle=B_{r}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\quad\text{with }r>0

\displaystyle\text{upper half-ball of radius }r,

\displaystyle\vec{\varepsilon}_{1}

\displaystyle,\ldots,\vec{\varepsilon}_{m}

\displaystyle\text{canonical basis of }\mathbb{R}^{m}.

We will write

\partial B_{r}^{+}(0)=rI+rS,

where

\displaystyle rI

\displaystyle=\{(x^{1},0):-r<x^{1}<r\}\simeq(-r,r)

\displaystyle\text{base diameter},

\displaystyle rS

\displaystyle=\{(x^{1},\sqrt{1-(x^{1})^{2}}):-r<x^{1}<r\}

\displaystyle\text{upper semi-circle}.

When
\Omega
is a domain of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and

f=(f^{1},\ldots,f^{n}):\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
is a given function, we adopt the following notation:

\nabla f=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}f\\
\partial_{2}f\end{pmatrix},\qquad~~~\nabla^{\perp}f=\begin{pmatrix}-\partial_{2}f\\
\partial_{1}f\end{pmatrix},

and if
g:\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is another function, we set

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{\perp}f,g\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\langle\partial_{2}f,g\rangle\\
\langle\partial_{1}f,g\rangle\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{\perp}f,\nabla g\rangle

\displaystyle=\divop(\langle\nabla^{\perp}f,g\rangle)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\nabla^{\perp}f^{i},\nabla g^{i}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{1}f^{i}\partial_{2}g^{i}-\partial_{2}f^{i}\partial_{1}g^{i}.

1.2
Geometric notions

We list here the geometric objects that will appear in the paper.
For more information, we refer the reader
to textbooks of differential geometry of curves and surfaces
such as
[
1
]
,
[
44
]
and
[
8
]

and to the monograph
[
59
]
.
For a given immersion
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)}\to(\mathbb{R}^{m},\langle\;,\;\rangle)

into the Euclidean
m
-dimensional space, we denote:

-

the induced metric on
B_{1}(0)
:

g_{\Phi}=(g_{\Phi})_{ij}=(\langle\partial_{i}\Phi,\partial_{j}\Phi\rangle)_{ij}
,
with inverse
(g_{\Phi}^{ij})_{ij}
,

-

the induced area element:
\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}=\sqrt{\det g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}x
,

-

the Gauss map:

\vec{n}_{\Phi}:B_{1}(0)\to\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
defined as

\displaystyle\vec{n}_{\Phi}(x)=\star\frac{\partial_{1}\Phi(x)\wedge\partial_{2}\Phi(x)}{|\partial_{1}\Phi(x)\wedge\partial_{2}\Phi(x)|},

(1.10)

where
\star
is the Hodge operator in
\mathbb{R}^{m}

(here
\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
denotes the
(m-2)
-dimensional Grassmannian of
\mathbb{R}^{m}

and
\star
is the Hodge operator in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
see e.g.
[
8
, Chapter 2]
)
3
3

3

when
m=3
, we have the canonical identification

\star(V\wedge W)=V\times W
, where
\times
is the vector product,
so in this case
\vec{n}_{\Phi}
can be considered as a
S^{2}
-valued map.
.
It can be represented as

\vec{n}_{\Phi}=\vec{n}_{\Phi,1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\vec{n}_{\Phi,m-2}
,
where the
\vec{n}_{\Phi,i}
are normal vector fields for
\Phi
so
that the
m
-ple

(\partial_{1}\Phi,\partial_{2}\Phi,\vec{n}_{\Phi,1},\ldots,\vec{n}_{\Phi,m-2})

defines a positively oriented basis of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

-

the Hessian:
\nabla^{2}\Phi
,
and its norm:

|\nabla^{2}\Phi|_{g_{\Phi}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}g_{\Phi}^{ik}g_{\Phi}^{jl}\langle\partial^{2}_{ij}\Phi,\partial^{2}_{kl}\Phi\rangle,

-

the second fundamental form:
\vec{\II}_{\Phi}=\pi_{\vec{n}_{\Phi}}(\nabla^{2}\Phi)
,
where
\pi_{\vec{n}_{\Phi}}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the normal bundle of
\Phi
,
and its norm:

|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|_{g_{\Phi}}^{2}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}g_{\Phi}^{ik}g_{\Phi}^{jl}\langle\vec{\II}_{ij},\vec{\II}_{kl}\rangle,

-

the Gauss curvature:
K_{\Phi}
and the mean curvature vector:
\vec{H}_{\Phi}
,

-

the area functional:

\Area(\Phi)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}
,

-

the total curvature functional:

E(\Phi)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}
.

When
\Phi
is conformal with conformal factor
\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{\Phi}}=|\nabla\Phi|/\sqrt{2}
,
some of the above mentioned objects can be written in an easier way in that

\displaystyle(g_{\Phi})_{ij}=\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{\Phi}}\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle(g_{\Phi})^{ij}=\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{\Phi}}\delta^{ij},

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}=\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}=\mathrm{e}^{-4\lambda_{\Phi}}|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2},

\displaystyle|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}=\mathrm{e}^{-4\lambda_{\Phi}}|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}.

A direct computation reveals that the general identity

|\nabla^{2}\Phi|_{g_{\Phi}}=|\pi_{T_{\Phi}}(\nabla^{2}\Phi)|_{g_{\Phi}}+|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}},

where
\pi_{T_{\Phi}}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the tangent bundle of
\Phi
,
can be rewritten, when
\Phi
is conformal, as

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}

\displaystyle=4\mathrm{e}^{-2\lambda_{\Phi}}|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}|^{2}+|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}.

(1.11)

If
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a simple, closed curve
with a chosen arc-length parametrization
\gamma:[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim\to\Gamma

and
\vec{n}_{0}=\vec{n}_{0}(\gamma(\cdot))
is some unit-normal
(m-2)
-vector field
along
\Gamma
,
the geodesic curvature
k_{g}
of
\Gamma
(with respect to
\vec{n}_{0})

is defined as follows:
if
\mathbf{t}=\dot{\gamma}

denotes the unit-tangent vector of
\Gamma
we set
(see
[
1
, Chapter 5]
)

\displaystyle k_{g}=\left\langle\dot{\mathbf{t}},\star(\vec{n}_{0}\wedge\mathbf{t})\right\rangle=\left\langle\ddot{\gamma},\star(\vec{n}_{0}\wedge\dot{\gamma})\right\rangle.

(1.12)

Definition 1.5

(Weak notions of immersions)
.

(i)

\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}
is the set of
Lipschitz maps
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

so that there exists a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism

\psi:B_{1}(0)\to B_{1}(0)
so that
\Psi:=\Phi\circ\psi
is conformal and
there exists a finite (possibly empty) set
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}\subset\overline{B_{1}(0)}

so that, for every compact set
K\subset\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
there
is a constant
C_{K}>0
so that there holds

\displaystyle\sqrt{\det{g_{\Psi}}}=|\nabla\Psi|^{2}/2\geq C_{K}\quad\text{a.e. in }K,

(1.13)

and if the Gauss map of
\Psi
, defined a.e. as in (
1.10
)
belongs to
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
.

(ii)

If
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a simple, closed curve and

\vec{n}_{0}
is a unit-normal
(m-2)
-vector field
along
\Gamma
,

\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

is the set of Lipschitz maps
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
so that

\Phi
is an element of
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}

and for some homeomorphism

\sigma_{\Phi}:S^{1}\to[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim
there holds

\displaystyle\Phi(x)=\gamma(\sigma_{\Phi}(x))\quad\text{and}\quad\vec{n}_{\Phi}(x)=\vec{n}_{0}(\sigma_{\Phi}(x)),\quad\text{for }x\text{ in }\partial B_{1}(0)=S^{1},

(1.14)

where
\gamma:[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim\to\Gamma

is a fixed arc-length parametrization of
\Gamma
.

(iii)

For
A>0
,
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

is the set of Lipschitz maps
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

so that
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

and

\Area(\Phi)=A.

For
\Phi
as in definition
1.5
,
we may write:

\displaystyle E(\Phi)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}=\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi\circ\psi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}x<+\infty,

where
\psi:B_{1}(0)\to B_{1}(0)
is the homeomorphism giving the conformal
parametrization of
\Phi
.

If
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a possibly branched conformal immersion,
the logarithm of its conformal factor
\lambda_{\Phi}=\log(|\nabla\Phi|/\sqrt{2})

will be a weak solution of the so-called Liouville equation:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\lambda_{\Phi}&=K_{\Phi}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{\Phi}}-2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{N}n_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}&&\text{in}\,B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{\Phi}&=k_{g}(\sigma_{\Phi})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{\Phi}}-1&&\text{on}\,\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.15)

where

a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
are the branch points of
\Phi
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
are their respective multiplicities.

The relative abundance of maps satisfying
definition
1.5
is
implied by the following result which makes use
Müller-Šverák theory of weak isothermic
charts
[
52
]
and Hélein’s moving frame technique
(for more deails we refer to
[
38
]
and
[
59
]
):

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
\Phi\in W^{1,\infty}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfy the following conditions:

i)

there exists some
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|\nabla\Phi|^{2}\leq C\sqrt{\det g_{\Phi}}\quad\text{a.e. in }B_{1}(0),

ii)

there exists a finite (possibly empty) set
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}\subset B_{1}(0)

such that for every compact set
K\subset\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
there holds

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\geq C_{K},

for some constant
C_{K}>0
,

iii)

its Gauss map, defined a.e. as in (
1.10
),
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j=1,2\\
l=1,\ldots,m-2\end{subarray}}g_{\Phi}^{ij}\langle\partial_{i}\vec{n}_{\Phi,l},\partial_{j}\vec{n}_{\Phi,l}\rangle\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}<+\infty.

Then there is a bi-Lipschitz diffeomorphism
\psi:B_{1}(0)\to B_{1}(0)

so that
\Phi\circ\psi
is conformal and
\Phi\circ\psi\in W^{2,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

Some further information about moving frames is
in appendix
A.3
.

The paper is organised as follows:

-

in section
2
we prove a priori estimate
on boundary exponential intergrability for the Neumann problem that will
be needed in the sequel;

-

in section
3

we prove the first part of theorem
1.2
, i.e. the existence of a Lipschitz minimising immersion,

-

in section
4
we prove an
\varepsilon
-regularity result up to the boundary for minimisers by constructing suitable
competitors that permits us to conclude the proof of
theorems
1.2
-
1.3
.

2
An Estimate for the Neumann Problem

For given
f\in L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
,
g\in L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))

we recall that a function
u\in W^{1,1}(B_{1}(0))

is said to weakly solve the Neumann problem for the Poisson equation in
B_{1}(0)
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=f&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=g&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1)

if, for every
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
,
there holds

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)}f\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g\,\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\langle\nabla u,\nabla\psi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x.

From this expression, it is immediate to see that a necessary condition for
the existence of a weak solution is that
\int_{B_{1}(0)}f=-\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g

(see e.g.
[
40
]
for more on weak formulations of Neumann problems).
Such condition is also sufficient and we have the following representation formula:

\displaystyle u(x)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}u\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)\,f(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)g(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y)\quad x\in B_{1}(0),

where
\mathcal{G}
is the
Green function for the Neumann problem (with zero average on
\partial B_{1}(0)
),
that is the function:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}(\log|x-y|+\log|\tilde{x}-y||x|)+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{4\pi},

(2.2)

which satisfies, for every
x\in B_{1}(0)
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta_{y}\mathcal{G}(x,\cdot)&=\delta_{x}-\frac{1}{|D|},&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu_{y}}\mathcal{G}(x,\cdot)&=0&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\\
\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y)&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.3)

Two such solutions to (
2.1
) differ by a constant.

In what follows, we denote
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\phi
by
\overline{\phi}
.
The results we now present are in the spirit of Brezis-Merle
[
11
, Theorem 1]

and Da Lio-Martinazzi-Rivière
[
15
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
,
g\in L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))
satisfy

\int_{B_{1}(0)}f=-\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g
and let
u\in W^{1,1}(B_{1}(0))
be a weak solution
to the problem
(
2.1
).
Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
verifying

\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}<\pi-\varepsilon

we have

\|\mathrm{e}^{u-\overline{u}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq C_{\varepsilon},

for some
C_{\varepsilon}>0
depending on
\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle p=\frac{\pi-\varepsilon}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

Set
k=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{B_{1}(0)}f=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g
.
We write the solution of (
2.1
) as:

\displaystyle u(x)-\overline{u}=u_{1}(x)+u_{2}(x)+\frac{k}{2}(1-|x|^{2}),\quad x\in B_{1}(0),

(2.4)

where:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{1}&=f-2k,&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{1}&=0&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\\
\overline{u_{1}}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{2}&=0,&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{2}&=g+k&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\\
\overline{u_{2}}&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Step 1: study of
u_{1}
.

We set
F=f-2k=f-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(0)}f
;
note that there holds

\int_{B_{1}(0)}F=0

and

\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}\leq 2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}
.
The Green function for the Neumann problem
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\log\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)+\log\left(\frac{2}{|\tilde{x}-y||x|}\right)\right)+\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{4\pi}-\frac{1}{\pi}\log 2,

where,
since
|x-y|\leq 2
and
|\tilde{x}-y||x|\leq 2
,
the term in brackets is non negative.
Then we may write
u_{1}
as:

\displaystyle u_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{B_{1}(0)}\left(\log\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)+\log\left(\frac{2}{|\tilde{x}-y||x|}\right)\right)F(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\pi}F(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,

and in particular, for
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)
, we have the formula:

\displaystyle u_{1}(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B_{1}(0)}\log\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)F(y)\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{B_{1}(0)}\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\pi}F(y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\partial B_{1}(0).

For
\gamma>0
then there holds:

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma|u_{1}(x)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\leq\frac{\gamma}{\pi}\int_{B_{1}(0)}\log\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)\frac{|F(y)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\,\mathrm{d}y+\frac{\gamma}{4\pi},

so by Jensen’s inequality (see e.g.
[
29
, Appendix B]
):

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\gamma|u_{1}(x)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\right)\leq\int_{B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\pi}}\frac{|F(y)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\,\mathrm{d}y\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}}.

Integrating on
\partial B_{1}(0)
and using Tonelli’s theorem
yields:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma|u_{1}(x)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)\leq\int_{B_{1}(0)}\left\{\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\pi}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)\right\}\frac{|F(y)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\,\mathrm{d}y\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\gamma}{4\pi}}.

Then one sees that,
for
\gamma<\pi
, the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{|x-y|}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{\pi}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)

is convergent and its value uniformly bounded in
y\in B_{1}(0)
.
We conclude that:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma|u_{1}(x)|}{\|F\|_{L^{1}(D)}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)\leq C_{\gamma},\quad\text{for }\gamma<\pi,

(2.5)

for some constant
C_{\gamma}>0
depending on
\gamma.

Step 2: study of
u_{2}
.

Note that for
x=\mathrm{e}^{i\phi}
,
y=\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}
,
\mathcal{G}(x,y)

takes the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{e}^{i\phi},\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\log|x-y|=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log(2(1-\cos(\phi-\theta)))=:G(\phi-\theta),

(2.6)

hence the boundary value of
u_{2}
can be written as:

\displaystyle u_{2}(\phi)=u_{2}(\mathrm{e}^{i\phi})=G*(g+k)=G*g,\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),

where the last equality follows since
G
has zero average.
Using again this property, we may write:

\displaystyle u_{2}(\mathrm{e}^{i\phi})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\log(2(1-\cos(\phi-\theta)))g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\log\left(\frac{1-\cos(\phi-\theta)}{2}\right)g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where now the argument in the logarithm is always bigger than
1
.
As in step 1, for
\gamma>0
and Jensen’s inequality
one deduces:

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{\gamma u_{1}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}}\right)\leq\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{1-\cos(\phi-\theta)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}}\frac{|g(\theta)|}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}}\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

hence with Tonelli’s theorem:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma u_{1}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left\{\int_{\partial_{B_{1}(0)}}\left(\frac{2}{1-\cos(\phi-\theta)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi\right\}\frac{|g(\theta)|}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}}\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

Provided
\gamma<\pi
the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{1-\cos(\phi-\theta)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi=\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\left(\frac{2}{1-\cos(\phi)}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}}\,\mathrm{d}\phi

is convergent, hence
we conclude that:

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\exp\left(\frac{\gamma|u_{2}(x)|}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{\gamma},\quad\text{for }\gamma<\pi,

(2.7)

and for some
C_{\gamma}>0
.

Step 3.

Finally from (
2.4
),
we may write

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{u-\overline{u}}=\mathrm{e}^{u_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{u_{2}}\quad\text{on }\partial_{B_{1}(0)}.

In particular, if
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}
, by Hölder’s inequality
there holds

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{u-\overline{u}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial(B_{1}(0)))}\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{u_{1}}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|\mathrm{e}^{u_{2}}\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\partial B_{1}(0))}.

Choosing

\displaystyle p_{1}=\frac{\pi-\varepsilon}{2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}}\quad\text{and}\quad p_{2}=\frac{\pi-\varepsilon}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}},

we reach the conclusion by using estimates
(
2.5
) and (
2.7
) with
\gamma=\pi-\varepsilon
.
This proves theorem
2.1
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

In step 2,
G
defined in (
2.6
) has zero average, the computation is invariant
by translations of
g
.
Consequently, the assumption on
f
and
g
on theorem
2.1

may be replaced by

\displaystyle\|g-\alpha\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}<\pi-\varepsilon\quad\text{for some }\alpha\in\mathbb{R}.

The following is a localised version of
theorem
2.1
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
,
g\in L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))
,

a_{1},\ldots a_{N}
be points in
B_{1}(0)

and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}
be real numbers
satisfying
\int_{B_{1}(0)}f+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g
.
Let
u\in W^{1,1}(B_{1}(0))
be a weak solution to the problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=f+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=g&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0).\end{aligned}\right.

(2.8)

Assume that, for a given
x_{0}\in\partial B_{1}(0)
and
0<r<1
,

B_{r}(x_{0})\cap\{a_{1},\ldots a_{N}\}=\emptyset
and

\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}+2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}<\pi-\varepsilon
, for some
0<\varepsilon<\pi
.
Then

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{u-\overline{u}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\leq C_{\varepsilon}\,C_{1}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{C_{2}},

(2.9)

where

\displaystyle p=\frac{\pi-\varepsilon}{\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}+2\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}},

C_{\varepsilon}>0
is a constant depending on
\varepsilon
and
C_{1},C_{2}
depend on
\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))},

\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))})
and the
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}s
.

Proof of Lemma
2.3
.

Let
\chi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}
be a function in
C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
so that

\chi=1
in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{3r/4}(x_{0})
and with support in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
.
We write the solution of (
2.8
) as:

u-\overline{u}=u_{1}+u_{2},

where:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{1}&=f\chi&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{1}&=g\chi-c&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\\
\overline{u}_{1}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{2}&=f(1-\chi)+\sum_{i-1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{2}&=g(1-\chi)+c&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\\
\overline{u}_{2}&=0,\end{aligned}\right.

with
c=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{B_{1}(0)}f\chi+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}g\chi
.
Applying theorem

2.1
together with remark
2.2
,
we deduce the existence of
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{u_{1}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq C_{\varepsilon}.

(2.10)

To estimate
u_{2}
we use the representation formula

\displaystyle u_{2}(x)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)f(y)(1-\chi(y))\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)g(y)(1-\chi(y))\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{G}(x,a_{i}).

Notice that for
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)
we have

\mathrm{e}^{\alpha_{i}\mathcal{G}(x,a_{i})}=|x-a_{i}|^{-\alpha_{i}/\pi}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha_{i}(|a_{i}|^{2}-1)/4\pi}
.
Since none of the
a_{i}
’s is in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
we have

r/2\leq|x-a_{i}|\leq 2
for
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0})
and

|x-a_{i}|^{-\alpha_{i}/\pi}\lesssim r^{-|\alpha_{i}|/\pi}
. Observe also that
and
1-\chi
vanishes in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{3r/4}(x_{0})
, therefore for
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0})
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{|u_{2}(x)|}\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{C_{2}}

(2.11)

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
depend on
\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))},

\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))})
and

\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\alpha_{i}|
.
Hence
joining estimates (
2.10
) and (
2.11
),
we then deduce the validity of (
2.9
).
This proves the lemma.
∎

3
Existence of a Minimiser for the Total Curvature Energy

This section is devoted to prove the main part of theorem
1.2
, namely the existence for an admissible
triple
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
of a minimiser for the total curvature energy
(
1.2
) in the class
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
introduced in definition

1.5
.
In the section
4
it is shown that such a minimiser is actually
C^{1}
and not only Lipschitz .

We need several preliminary lemmas.
Along this section,
\Gamma
and
\vec{n}_{0}
will be fixed as in the statement of theorem

1.2
,

\gamma:[0,\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)]/\sim\to\Gamma
will denote
a fixed arc-length parametrization of
\Gamma

and
k_{g}
its geodesic curvature defined in (
1.12
).
When dealing with a sequence of maps
(\Phi)_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
,
we denote with a subscript
k
every quantity pertaining to the immersion
\Phi_{k}

(e.g. the Gauss map
\vec{n}_{\Phi_{k}}
will be simply denoted by
\vec{n}_{k}
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
be a sequence in of conformal maps in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

with

E=\sup_{k}E(\Phi_{k})<+\infty.

Then:

(i)

the
L^{1}
-norm of the Gauss curvature
\|K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}

is uniformly bounded,

(ii)

the number of branch points of
\Phi_{k}
and their multiplicity
is uniformly bounded on
k
,

(iii)

The
L^{(2,\infty)}
-norm of the gradient of the conformal factor

\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}(B_{1}(0))}
is uniformly bounded on
k
,

(iv)

for any fixed
E_{0}>0
, the set of points
x\in\overline{B_{1}(0)}
such that the curvature energy concentrates
above the level
E_{0}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to+\infty}\left(\inf\{r>0:\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x))}\geq E_{0}\}\right)=0

is finite and its cardinality is uniformly bounded on
k
.

All such bounds depend only on
E

and on
\|k_{g}\|_{L^{1}(\Gamma)}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Proof of (i)
. The bound follows from the pointwise a.e. relation
(see appendix
A.3
for more details)

\displaystyle K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\leq\frac{|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}|^{2}}{2}.

(3.1)

Proof of (ii)
. Since
\lambda_{k}
is a weak solution to the Liouville’s equation

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\lambda_{k}&=K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}-2\pi\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}n_{i_{k}}\delta_{a_{i_{k}}}&&\text{in}\,B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}\lambda_{k}&=k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}-1&&\text{on}\,\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(3.2)

there must hold

\displaystyle-2\pi\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}n_{i_{k}}+\int_{B_{1}(0)}K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}(k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}-1)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1},

hence

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}|n_{i_{k}}|\leq C\big(\|K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\|k_{g}\|_{L^{1}(\Gamma)}+1\big),

and the result then follows from (i).

Proof of (iii)
. Using Green’s representation formula
(the green function
\mathcal{G}
is given in (
2.3
)),
we have

\displaystyle\nabla\lambda_{k}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}(x,y)K_{k}(y)\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}(y)}\,\mathrm{d}y-2\pi\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}n_{i_{k}}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}(x,a_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}+\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}(x,y)k_{g}(\sigma_{k}(y))\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}(y)}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y),

and since there holds

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in B_{1}(0)}\|\nabla_{x}\mathcal{G}(\cdot,y)\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}(B_{1}(0))}<+\infty,

we can estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\big(\|K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}|n_{i_{k}}|+\|k_{g}\|_{L^{1}(\Gamma)}+1\big),

(3.3)

hence deduce that the right-hand-side of (
3.3
) does not depend on
k
thanks to (i) and (ii).

Proof of (iv).

The proof of (iv) follows from standard concentration-compactness arguments and we omit it.
∎

Combining lemmas
A.9
and
3.1
,
the following estimate
is obtained for points in
B_{1}(0)
(see
[
59
, Theorem 4.5]
).

Lemma 3.2
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
with the following property.
Let
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of conformal maps in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

with

E=\sup_{k}E(\Phi_{k})<+\infty

and let
x_{0}\in B_{1}(0)
and
0<r<1
be so that

\overline{B_{r}(x_{0})}\subset B_{1}(0)
.
If, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\overline{B_{r}(x_{0})}
contains no branch points of
\Phi_{k}

and there holds

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}^{2}\leq\varepsilon

(3.4)

for some
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
then
\lambda_{k}\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{r}(x_{0})})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{r}(x_{0}))
and there exist a constant

C=C(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},E,\varepsilon_{0},r)>0

and a sequence
(c_{k})_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}

so that

\displaystyle\sup_{k}\bigg(\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}+\|\lambda_{k}-c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\bigg)\leq C.

(3.5)

We have the following analogue result for boundary points.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
with the following property.
Let
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of conformal maps in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

with

E=\sup_{k}E(\Phi_{k})<+\infty,

and let
x_{0}\in\partial B_{1}(0)
and
0<r<1
.
If, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
B_{1}(0)\cap\overline{B_{r}(x_{0})}
contains no branch points of
\Phi_{k}

and, having denoted
\vec{e}_{k}=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{k}}(\partial_{1}\Phi_{k},\partial_{2}\Phi_{k})

the ortho-normal frame
associated with
\Phi_{k}
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}^{2}+\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad[\vec{e}_{k}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}^{2}\leq\varepsilon

(3.6)

for some
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
then

\lambda_{k}\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))

and there exist constant a constant

C=C(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},E,\varepsilon_{0},r)>0

independent of
\varepsilon

and a sequence
(c_{k})_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}

so that

\displaystyle\sup_{k}\bigg(\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0}))}+\|\lambda_{k}-c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0}))}\bigg)\leq C.

(3.7)

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

On the one hand,
from the pointwise a.e. relation
(
3.1
)
and (
3.6
)
we have

\displaystyle\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}+2\|K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon

consequently since
\lambda_{k}
is a weak solution to Liouville’s equation
(
3.2
),
by choosing an
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough,
by lemma
2.3

and lemma
3.1

we may find a
p=p(\varepsilon_{0})>1
so that,
uniformly on
k
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}-\overline{\lambda}_{k}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\leq C.

(3.8)

On the other hand, possibly after reducing
\varepsilon_{0}

we can invoke lemma
A.11

and deduce the existence of Coulomb ortho-normal frames

\vec{g}_{k}=(\vec{g}_{k,1},\vec{g}_{k,2})\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))

lifting
\vec{n}_{k}
in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
,
coinciding with
\vec{e}_{k}
on
\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})

and so that uniformly in
k
there holds

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{g}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq C.

(3.9)

In particular, we may write:

\displaystyle K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{g}_{k,1},\nabla\vec{g}_{k,2}\rangle\text{ in }B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}-1

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{\tau}\vec{g}_{k,1},\vec{g}_{k,2}\rangle\text{ on }\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}).

From lemma
A.8
,
we deduce that
\lambda_{k}\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0})})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0}))

and that for some constant
c_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
there holds

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{k}-c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0}))}{+\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(x_{0}))}}

(3.10)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\leq C\bigg(\|K_{k}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{N_{k}}|\alpha_{i_{k}}|

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+\|\nabla\vec{g}_{k,1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\|\vec{g}_{k,2}\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{g}_{k,1}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\|\vec{g}_{k,2}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}\bigg).

The first line on the right hand side of (
3.10
) can be estimated
uniformly on
k
by means of lemma
3.1
-(i)-(ii).
The second line can be estimated
uniformly on
k
with (
3.9
).
Finally the third line is estimated
uniformly on
k
with (
3.8
)
since, for
i=1,2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\vec{g}_{k,i}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|(|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})|+|\dot{\vec{n}}_{0}(\sigma_{k})|)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}+1\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\big(\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}-\overline{\lambda}_{k}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}+1\big).

(3.11)

In (
3
) we use the fact that
k_{g}(\sigma_{k}),\dot{\vec{n}}_{0}(\sigma_{k})\in L^{\infty}(\partial{B_{1}(0)}
by the current assumptions on the boundary data and that by Jensen’s inequality it holds uniformly on
k
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\overline{\lambda}_{k}}=\exp\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\partial{B_{1}(0)}}\lambda_{k}\leq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\partial{B_{1}(0)}}\exp(\lambda_{k})=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)}{2\pi}.

(3.12)

This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Definition 3.4
.

Let
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
be three distinct, fixed, consecutive points
in
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
on
\Gamma

that is,
\gamma(s_{j})=P_{j}
for some
0\leq s_{1}<s_{2}<s_{3}<\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)
.
We denote by

\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
is the set of maps
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

so that

\displaystyle\Phi\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}j}\right)=P_{j}\quad\text{for }j=1,2,3.

(3.13)

Remark 3.5
.

We note that:

(i)

if
\sigma_{\Phi}
defines the boundary parametrization of
\Phi
,
that is
\Phi|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\gamma\circ\sigma_{\Phi}
,
condition (
3.13
) is equivalent to

\sigma_{\Phi}\left(\frac{2\pi}{3}j\right)=s_{j}
for
j=1,2,3
.

(ii)

For every
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
,
there is a unique Möbius transformation of
B_{1}(0)
so that

\Phi\circ\phi\in\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
.
Moreover, the invariance by diffeomorphisms of the total curvature energy
implies that
E(\Phi)=E(\Phi\circ\phi)
, hence

\displaystyle\inf_{\Phi\in\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)}E(\Phi)=\inf_{\Psi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)}E(\Psi).

The following lemma is a consequence of the Courant-Lebesgue lemma,
a key tool in the analysis of Plateau’s problem (see
e.g.
[
14
, Lemma 4.14]
).

Lemma 3.6
.

For any sequence
(\Phi_{k})_{k}\subset\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
,
the sequence of boundary curves
(\Phi_{k}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)})_{k}
is equicontinuous.

Equicontinuity of the boundary curves is equivalent to the equicontinuity of the
\sigma_{k}
’s.
As a consequence of lemma
3.6
, we have:

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

and let
x_{0}\in\partial B_{1}(0)
be fixed.
Then, possibly passing to a subsequence, for any any
\varepsilon>0
,
there always exists an
r=r(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})>0
so that:

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\,[\vec{e}_{k}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon.

(3.14)

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Possibly after extracting a subsequence, thanks to
lemma
3.6
and the Arzelà-Ascoli
theorem, we may suppose that
\sigma_{k}
converges uniformly on
\partial B_{1}(0)
to some
continuous map
\sigma
.
As a consequence,
we have the pointwise convergence away from the diagonal:

\displaystyle\lim_{k}\left(\frac{\sigma_{k}(\theta_{1})-\sigma_{k}(\theta_{2})}{\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}}-\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{2}}}\right)=\frac{\sigma(\theta_{1})-\sigma(\theta_{2})}{\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}}-\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{2}}}\quad\text{for }\theta_{1}\neq\theta_{2},

and, possibly after extracting another subsequence,
the bound:

\displaystyle\frac{|\sigma_{k}(\theta_{1})-\sigma_{k}(\theta_{2})|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}}-\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{2}}|^{2}}\leq 2\frac{|\sigma(\theta_{1})-\sigma(\theta_{2})|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{1}}-\mathrm{e}^{i\theta_{2}}|^{2}}.

Hence, integrating both sides,
we deduce that for every
\rho>0
and
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)

there holds:

\displaystyle[\sigma_{k}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{\rho}(x))}^{2}\leq 2[\sigma]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{\rho}(x))}^{2}.

(3.15)

Let us assume without loss of generality that
x_{0}=1
and that
r<1
, so that we
may identify
[-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}]\simeq\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})

for some
0<\theta_{0}<\pi
.
Writing in complex notation

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}(\vec{e}_{k,1}+i\vec{e}_{k,2})=\star(\mathbf{t}\wedge\vec{n}_{0})(\sigma_{k})+i\mathbf{t}(\sigma_{k})\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),

thanks to (
3.15
)
we deduce that

4
4

4

Recall the elementary inequality:

\displaystyle[ab]_{W^{1/2,2}}^{2}\leq 2\left(\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}[b]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}}+\|b\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}[a]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}}\right).

Also recall that if
a:(l_{1},l_{2})\to\mathbb{C}
is a Lipschitz function
and
b:(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})\to(l_{1},l_{2})
is a function in
H^{1/2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))

we have:

\displaystyle[a\circ b]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq[a]_{C^{0,1}((l_{1},l_{2}))}^{2}[b]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}.

that

\displaystyle[\vec{e}_{k}]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left([\mathbf{t}(\sigma_{k})]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}+[\star(\mathbf{t}\wedge\vec{n}_{0})(\sigma_{k})]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}\right)+4\theta_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left([\mathbf{t}]^{2}_{C^{0,1}}+[\star(\mathbf{t}\wedge\vec{n}_{0})]^{2}_{C^{0,1}}\right)[\sigma_{k}]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}+4\theta_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left([\sigma]^{2}_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}+\theta_{0}^{2}\right),

and so the first inequality in
(
3.14
)
follow by choosing a sufficiently small
\theta_{0}
.
As for the second inequality in (
3.14
), if
a
and
b
denote the extrema of

\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
, from the point-wise convergence of
\sigma_{k}
to
\sigma

we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{k}\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})}|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})}\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\lim_{k}\int_{\sigma_{k}(a)}^{\sigma_{k}(b)}|k_{g}(s)||\dot{\gamma}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\sigma(a)}^{\sigma(b)}|k_{g}(s)||\dot{\gamma}(s)|\,\mathrm{d}s,

hence, possibly after extracting a subsequence, there holds:

\displaystyle\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\sigma_{k}^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq 2\|k_{g}(\sigma)\sigma^{\prime}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))},

and the results then follows by choosing

r
sufficiently small.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Definition 3.8

(Weak Sequential convergence)
.

Given a sequence
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
of conformal maps in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0})
,
and a conformal map
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
we say that
\Phi_{k}

weakly converges
to
\Phi
if:

(i)

\Phi_{k}\rightharpoonup\Phi\quad\text{in }W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and a.e. on
B_{1}(0)
,

(ii)

\Phi_{k}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\to\Phi|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\quad\text{uniformly in }C^{0}(\partial B_{1}(0)),

(iii)

\nabla\lambda_{k}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\nabla\lambda\text{ in }L^{(2,\infty)}(B_{1}(0)),

and there exists a finite, possibly empty set
\underline{\eta}=\{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{N}\}\subset\overline{B_{1}(0)}

so that, for every open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with compact closure in

\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\underline{\eta}
, there holds:

(iv)

\lambda_{k}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\lambda\text{ in }L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{n})

and ,

(v)

\Phi_{k}\rightharpoonup\Phi\quad\text{in }W^{2,2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{n}),

where
\lambda=\log(|\nabla\Phi|/\sqrt{2})
.

We are now in the position to prove the following compactness result.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
be a sequence
of conformal maps in
\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

with

\sup_{k}E(\Phi_{k})<+\infty.

Then
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
contains a subsequence weakly converging in the sense of
definition
3.8

to an element
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}^{*}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Step 1.

Since for every
k
we have that
\|\nabla\Phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}=2A

and the three-point condition (
3.13
) holds,
we deduce that
\sup_{k}(\|\Phi_{k}\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))})
is finite and so by the Rellich-Kondrachov
theorem, possibly passing to a subsequence, condition
(i)

of definition
3.8
is satisfied.

Step 2.

From lemma
3.6
,
by Arzelà-Ascoli theorem we deduce that, possibly passing to a subsequence, condition
(ii)

of definition
3.8
is satisfied.

Step 3.

From lemma
3.1
-(iii),
we deduce that, possibly passing to a subsequence, condition
(iii)

of definition
3.8
is satisfied.

Step 4.

If
\{a_{k,1},\ldots a_{k,N_{k}}\}
is the set of branch points
of
\Phi_{k}
,
from lemma
3.1
,
possibly after extracting a subsequence, we may suppose that
N_{k}

is independent of
k
and that, for each
j=1,\ldots N
,

\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k,j}=a_{j}
for some
a_{j}\in\overline{B_{1}(0)}
.

We say that a point
p
belongs to
\underline{\eta}
if either:

•

p=a_{k}
for some
k=1,\ldots,N
, or

•

there holds

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left(\inf\left\{r>0:\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D\cap B_{r}(p))}\geq\varepsilon_{0}\right\}\right)=0,

(3.16)

with
\varepsilon_{0}

is as in lemma
3.2
if
p\in B_{1}(0)
,
or
as in lemma
3.3
if
p\in\partial B_{1}(0)
, or

•

p=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}j}
for
j=1,2
or
3
.

Note that the set of points satisfying (
3.16
)
is, due to lemma
3.1
, finite and uniformly bounded in
k
.
Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
an open set with compact closure in

\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\underline{\eta}

and let
\Omega^{\prime}
be a closed set contained in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\underline{\eta}

and with smooth boundary
so that
\overline{\Omega}\subset\Omega^{\prime}
and for some small
\delta>0
there holds

\displaystyle\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\cup_{p\in\underline{\eta}}B_{2\delta}(p)\subset\subset\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\overline{B_{1}(0)}\setminus\cup_{p\in\underline{\eta}}B_{\delta}(p).

Possibly passing to a further subsequence,
we may suppose that, for every
k
,
the set of branch points
\{a_{k,1},\ldots a_{k,N_{k}}\}
of
\Phi_{k}
, lies in

\cup_{p\in\underline{\eta}}B_{\delta}(p)
.
Now, for every
x\in\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}
, we can choose an

r_{x}>0
so that,
if
x\in B_{1}(0)
, then
\overline{B_{r_{x}}(x)}\subset\Omega^{\prime}
and

\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{r_{x}}(x))}^{2}<\varepsilon_{0}
,
and,
if
x\in\partial B_{1}(0)
, then

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{x}}(x_{0}))}^{2}+\|k_{g}(\sigma_{k})\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{x}}(x))}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon_{0},

\displaystyle[\vec{e}_{k}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{x}}(x))}^{2}

\displaystyle<\varepsilon_{0},

(this can be done uniformly on
k
thanks to lemma
3.7
).
The family
\{B_{r_{x}/4}(x)\}_{x\in\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}}

forms an open cover of
\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}
,
from which we may extract a finite sub-cover

\{B_{r_{j}}(x_{j})\}_{j=1}^{M}
.
From lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
, we deduce
that
\lambda\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j})})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j}))
and there exists constants
l_{k}(x_{j})
, so that,
for
j=1,\ldots,M
,

\displaystyle\sup_{k}\bigg(\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j}))}+\|\lambda_{k}-l_{k}(x_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j}))}\bigg)\leq C.

(3.17)

Notice that, for every
i,j
the bound
|l_{k}(x_{i})-l_{k}(x_{j})|\leq MC
holds.
Indeed, if
B_{\rho}(x_{i})\cap B_{\rho}(x_{j})\neq\emptyset
, then from
(
3.17
) and the triangle inequality
we have

|l_{k}(x_{i})-l_{k}(x_{j})|\leq 2C.

and general
i
and
j
pick a collection from the covering which connects

x_{j}
and
x_{j}
and reach a similar conclusion.
We can then assume that such constants do not depend on
x_{j}
and consequently
that, for some
l_{k}
, there holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{k}\bigg(\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}+\|\lambda_{k}-l_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}\bigg)\leq C.

(3.18)

We claim that the sequence
(l_{k})_{k}
is uniformly bounded on
k
.
To see that
(l_{k})_{k}
is bounded from below, note that, if we had
\limsup_{k\to\infty}l_{k}=+\infty
,
possibly after extracting a subsequence
condition (
3.18
) would imply that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\lambda_{k}=+\infty

uniformly on
\Omega
.
Consequently we would have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}x=+\infty,

which contradicts condition
\|\nabla\Phi_{k}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}=2A
.
Suppose now
(l_{k})_{k}
is not bounded from above, that is
\liminf_{k\to\infty}l_{k}=+\infty
.
Let
\alpha
be an arbitrary closed, connected sub-arc of
\partial B_{1}(0)

which does not contain any point in
\underline{\eta}
.
We add if necessary a finite number of balls
B_{r_{j}}(x_{j})
, to the above finite cover of

\Omega^{\prime}

so that
\{B_{r_{j}}(x_{j})\}_{j}
also covers
\alpha
.
Since
\lambda_{k}
is continuous, possibly passing to a subsequence, from (
3.18
),
that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\lambda_{k}=-\infty
uniformly in
\alpha
.
Then,

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\int_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Phi_{k}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}(\alpha)),

and thus, by the weak lower-semicontinuity of the Hausdorff
measure with respect to the uniform convergence, that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Phi(\alpha))=0
.
Since the arc
\alpha
was arbitrarily chosen,
from the Borel-regularity of the Hausdorff measure (see
[
30
]
)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Phi(\partial B_{1}(0)\setminus\underline{\eta}))=0
,
but then,
\Phi
being continuous and
\underline{\eta}
consisting of a finite set,
that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Phi(\partial B_{1}(0)))=0
.
This contradicts the three-point normalization condition.
This proves that condition (
3.18
) can actually be strengthened to

\displaystyle\sup_{k}\bigg(\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}+\|\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}\bigg)\leq C.

(3.19)

and thus, possibly passing to a subsequence, condition (iv) of definition
3.8

is satisfied.

Step 5.

From (
3.19
), we can estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\Phi_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\|\Delta\Phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\mathrm{e}^{2\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}W(\Phi_{k})

\displaystyle\leq C,

(3.21)

moreover,

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\mathrm{e}^{\|\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})}}(1+\|\nabla\lambda_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega^{\prime})})\leq C,

hence

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\tau}\Phi_{k}\|_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\vec{e}_{k,1}(\sigma_{k})\|_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq C{}}+\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda_{k}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})}[\vec{e}_{k,1}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap\Omega^{\prime})})

\displaystyle\leq C.

From (
3.20
)-(
3.21
)-(
3.22
),
elliptic regularity theory yields
\sup_{k}\|\Phi_{k}\|_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}<+\infty
.
Thus, possibly passing to a subsequence, also condition (iv) of definition
3.8

holds.

Step 6.

As in
[
59
, Lemma 5.1]
,
we have that the Gauss map of
\Phi
extends to a map in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
,
and consequently, from lemma
A.14

in appendix
A.4
,
the structure near points of
\underline{\eta}
lying in
B_{1}(0)
is
that of a (possibly removable) branch point.
Finally as shown in lemma
A.15

singular points
a\in\underline{\eta}

lying on the boundary
are always removable,
and the limiting map
\Phi
extends to a conformal
Lipschitz immersion near
\partial B_{1}(0)
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The proof of the following lemma
can be easily deduced from its analogue in the closed case
(see
[
59
, Theorem 5.9]
).

Lemma 3.10
.

The total curvature energy functional
E

is sequentially lower semi-continuous in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

with respect to weak convergence in the sense of definition
3.8
,
that is,
if
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
is a sequence in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
weakly
converging to
\Phi
, then

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}E(\Phi_{k})\geq E(\Phi).

Proof of theorem
1.2
(first part).

Since
the triple
(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
is admissible,
the set
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
is not empty and
we can consider a sequence
(\Phi_{k})_{k}
in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

minimising the total curvature energy:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}E(\Phi_{k})=\inf\{E(\Psi):\Psi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)\}.

Thanks to lemma
1.6
,
and remark
3.5
,
we may assume that each
\Phi_{k}
is conformal
and satisfies the three-point normalisation condition given by
definition
3.4
.
From lemma
3.9
we can then extract a weakly converging subsequence
in the sense of definition
3.8

to a conformal map
\Phi
in

\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)
.
Finally, because of lemma
3.10
we have

\displaystyle E(\Phi)=\lim_{k\to\infty}E(\Phi_{k})=\inf\{E(\Psi):\Psi\in\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)\}.

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

4
Regularity of Minimisers

This section is devoted to prove
that any element in
\mathcal{F}_{B_{1}(0)}(\Gamma,\vec{n}_{0},A)

which minimises the total curvature energy satisfies all the regularity
statements of theorem
1.3
,
thereby also
concluding the proof of theorem
1.2
.

We need first some preparatory results regarding
immersions with
L^{2}
-bounded second fundamental form
and estimates on suitable competitors for the Poisson problem.
In this section, we denote
with
D\Area(\Phi)w
and
DE(\Phi)w

the directional derivative at
\Phi

along
w
of the area and
total curvature energy functional,
namely

\displaystyle D\Area(\Phi)w=\frac{\mathrm{d}\Area(\Phi+tw)}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{t=0},\quad DE(\Phi)w=\frac{\mathrm{d}E(\Phi+tw)}{\mathrm{d}t}\bigg|_{t=0},

and, if
\Omega
is the domain of
\Phi
(a ball or a half-ball) we set

\displaystyle\|D\Area(\Phi)\|:=\sup\{|D\Area(\Phi)w|:\,

\displaystyle w\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{m}),\,\|w\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1,\,\supp w\subset\subset\Omega\}.

4.1
Lemmas on Conformal Immersions

Lemma 4.1

(Interior Estimates for
\lambda_{\Phi}
)
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that, if

\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a conformal immersion
with
L^{2}
-bounded second fundamental form satisfying

\displaystyle E(\Phi)=\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}<\varepsilon_{0},

then,

(i)

for any
0<r<1
there holds

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(0)}|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\bigg(\frac{r^{2}}{2}+C\varepsilon_{0}\bigg)\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi},

(4.1)

(ii)

for any compact set
K\subset\subset{B_{1}(0)}
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{\Phi}-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,\partial{B_{1}(0)})^{2}}\|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}({B_{1}(0)})}+C\varepsilon_{0},

(4.2)

where
(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B_{1}(0)}\lambda_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d}x

denotes the average of
\lambda_{\Phi}
on
{B_{1}(0)}

and
C>0
is a constant independent of
\Phi
.

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Thanks to lemma
A.9
, if
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough
we may find a Coulomb orthonormal frame
\vec{f}
defined on
{B_{1}(0)}

satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}\leq C\|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}.

We write
\lambda_{\Phi}=\mu+h
, where
\mu

is the solution be the solution to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\mu&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{f}_{1},\nabla\vec{f}_{2}\rangle&&\text{in }{B_{1}(0)},\\
\mu&=0&&\text{on }\partial{B_{1}(0)},\end{aligned}\right.

and
h
is the harmonic rest.
By Wente’s lemma,
\mu
belongs to
C^{0}(\overline{B_{r}(0)})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{r}(0))

with

\displaystyle\|\nabla\mu\|_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}+\|\mu\|_{L^{\infty}({B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}.

(4.3)

As for
h
, since it is harmonic, for any
0<r<1
it satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(0)}|\nabla h|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq r^{2}\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla h|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(4.4)

Using successively (
4.4
),
the Dirichlet principle, estimate (
4.3
)
and identity (
1.11
) we then deduce

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(0)}|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r}(0)}|\nabla(\mu+h)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{2}\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla h|^{2}+2\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla\mu|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq 2r^{2}\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+C\|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}\|^{4}_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}+C\|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}\|^{4}_{L^{2}({B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{r^{2}}{2}+C\varepsilon_{0}\bigg)\bigg(\int_{{B_{1}(0)}}|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}\bigg),

which proves (
4.1
).
As for (
4.2
), we note that
h
may be written
by means of the Poisson kernel

5
5

5

the explicit formula is (see
[
29
, §2.2.4]
):

\displaystyle K(x,y)=\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2\pi}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}.

as

\displaystyle h(x)=\int_{\partial{B_{1}(0)}}K(x,y)\,\lambda(y)\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y),\quad x\in{B_{1}(0)},

consequently
we deduce that, for any
x\in K
,
using the trace theorem
and Poincaré’s inequality,
there holds

\displaystyle|h(x)-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{{B_{1}(0)}}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{\partial{B_{1}(0)}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{2}}|\lambda_{\Phi}(y)-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}|\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,\partial B_{1}(0))^{2}}\|\lambda_{\Phi}-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{1}(\partial{B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,\partial B_{1}(0))^{2}}\|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}\|_{L^{1}({B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,\partial B_{1}(0))^{2}}\|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}({B_{1}(0)})}.

We may then conclude with (
4.3
) that

\displaystyle\|\lambda_{\Phi}-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq\|h-(\lambda_{\Phi})_{B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\|\mu\|_{L^{\infty}({B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,\partial B_{1}(0))^{2}}\|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}\|_{L^{(2,\infty)}({B_{1}(0)})}+C\varepsilon_{0},

as desired.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.2

(Boundary Estimates for
\lambda_{\Phi}
)
.

There exists an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that the following holds.
Let
\Phi:B_{1}^{+}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a conformal immersion with

L^{2}
-bounded second fundamental form and let
\vec{e}=(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})

be its coordinate frame.
If for some
p>1
we have
\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}\in L^{p}(I,\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})

and

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}^{+}(0)}|\II_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(I)}^{2}<\varepsilon_{0},

(4.5)

then:

(i)

for any
0<r<1
there holds

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}^{+}(0)}|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\bigg(\frac{r^{2}}{2}\bigg)\int_{B_{1}^{+}(0)}|\nabla^{2}\Phi|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}

(4.6)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}+{}}+\left(C\varepsilon_{0}+C(p)\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{1}\|_{L^{p}(I)}(1+\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{2}\|_{L^{p}(I)})\right)\bigg(\int_{B_{1}^{+}(0)}|\vec{\II}_{\Phi}|^{2}_{g_{\Phi}}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}+\|\langle\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\|_{L^{1}(I)}\bigg),

(ii)

for any compact set
K\subset\overline{B_{1}^{+}(0)}

so that
\dist(K,S)>0
there holds

\displaystyle\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|\lambda_{\Phi}-c\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}\leq\frac{C}{\dist(K,S)^{2}}\|\nabla\lambda_{\Phi}\|_{L^{2,\infty}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}+C(p)(\varepsilon_{0}+\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{1}\|_{L^{p}(I)}(1+\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{2}\|_{L^{p}(I)})),

(4.7)

where
C>0
is an universal constant and

C(p)>0
is a constant depending only on
p
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Step 1.
Thanks to lemma
A.12
(see also remark
A.13
),
we can consider an extension
\vec{f}
of
\vec{e}|_{I}
to
all of
\partial B_{1}^{+}(0)
such that

\displaystyle[\vec{f}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}^{+}(0))}\leq 2[\vec{f}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(I)},

Step 2.
We choose
\varepsilon_{0}
sufficiently small so that thanks to lemma

A.10

we may find a frame
\vec{g}=(\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2})
lifting
\vec{n}_{\Phi}

on
B_{1}^{+}(0)
that coincides with
\vec{f}
on
\partial B_{1}^{+}(0)

and satisfies

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{g}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\nabla\vec{n}_{\Phi}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}+[\vec{f}]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}^{+}(0))}\right)\leq C\varepsilon_{0}.

Step 3.
We write
\lambda_{\Phi}=\mu+h
, where:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\mu&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{g}_{1},\nabla\vec{g}_{1}\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}^{+}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}\mu&=\langle\partial_{\tau}\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2}\rangle&&\text{on }I,\\
\mu&=0&&\text{on }S,\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta h&=0&&\text{in }B_{1}^{+}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}h&=0&&\text{on }I,\\
h&=\lambda&&\text{on }S.\end{aligned}\right.

Step 4: estimate of
\mu
.

Since
\mu
satisfies a homogeneous Dirichlet condition on
S
,
its extension to
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
by means of odd inversion along
S

(given by the conformal map
x\mapsto x/|x|^{2}
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}(x)=\begin{cases}\mu(x)&\text{for }x\in B_{1}^{+}(0),\\
-\mu(x/|x|^{2})&\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{1}^{+}(0),\end{cases}

satisties (also thanks to the transformation law under conformal maps of the Laplace operator and
of the determinant):

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\widehat{\mu}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1},\nabla\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}\rangle&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
\partial_{\nu}\widehat{\mu}&=\langle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1},\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}\rangle&&\text{on }\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\end{aligned}\right.

(4.8)

where for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{i}(x)=\begin{cases}\vec{g}_{i}(x)&\text{for }x\in B_{1}^{+}(0),\\
\vec{g}_{i}(x/|x|^{2})&\text{for }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\setminus B_{1}^{+}(0),\end{cases}

denote extensions of
\vec{g}_{i}
by means of even inversion along
S
.

We then consider the Cayley map

\phi(z)=i\frac{-z+1}{z+1}
mapping biholomorphically
B_{1}(0)
onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
and by simplicity of notation we continue to denote by

\widehat{\mu}
and
\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{i}
the composition
\widehat{\mu}\circ\phi
and
\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{i}\circ\phi.

By the conformal invariance of the problem (
4.9
),
\widehat{\mu}\circ\phi
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta\widehat{\mu}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1},\nabla\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}\widehat{\mu}&=\langle\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1},\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}\rangle&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(4.9)

Thanks to lemma
A.6

we have

\displaystyle\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|{\widehat{\mu}}-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}(1+\|\nabla{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))})

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\|\partial_{\tau}{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{1}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}(1+\|\partial_{\tau}{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{2}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}).

Because of the conformal invariance of the Dirichlet energy there holds

\displaystyle\|\nabla{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{i}}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}=\|\nabla\vec{g}_{i}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}\quad i=1,2,

on the other hand a direct computation shows that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\tau}{\widetilde{\vec{g}}_{i}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq 2\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{g}_{i}\|_{L^{p}(I)}\quad i=1,2,

hence we deduce (assuming without loss of generality that
\varepsilon_{0}<1

and recalling that
\vec{g}=\vec{e}
on
I
):

\displaystyle\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mu-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}\leq C\varepsilon_{0}+C(p)\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{1}\|_{L^{p}(I)}(1+\|\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{2}\|_{L^{p}(I)}).

(4.10)

Consequently, through integration by parts and by using (
4.10
) we can estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}^{+}(0)}|\nabla\mu|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{B_{1}^{+}(0)}(\mu-c)\Delta u\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{I}(\mu-c)\partial_{\nu}\lambda\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\leq\inf\|\mu-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}^{+}(0))}\bigg(\|\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{g}_{1},\nabla\vec{g}_{2}\rangle\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\|\langle\partial_{\tau}\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle\|_{L^{1}(I)}\bigg)
