satisfies the area-constrained
Willmore equation through the branch points,
and thus, from the analysis of singularities for Willmore
surfaces
[
57
,
7
,
41
,
42
]
,
this implies that
\Phi
is of class
C^{1,\alpha}
for every
0<\alpha<1

through the branch points.

We have thus proved that

\Phi
is of class
C^{1,\alpha}
-up to the boundary, for some
0<\alpha<1

(and accordingly the Gauss map
\vec{n}_{\Phi}
extends to a map of class

C^{0,\alpha}
-up to the boundary)
and this concludes the proof of the theorems.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Existence of Immersed Disks with Given Boundary Data

In this section we prove,
along with some other facts and comments,
the following result.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a simple, closed curve of class

C^{k,\alpha}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
\alpha\in(0,1]

whose unit tangent vector we denote by
\mathbf{t}

and let
\vec{n}_{0}
be a unit-normal
(m-2)
-vector field along

\Gamma
of class
C^{k,\alpha}
.
There exists a possibly branched immersed disk
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

of class
C^{k,\alpha}
and boundary
\Gamma
and
whose Gauss map along
\Gamma
is
\vec{n}_{0}
.
In particular, a branch-point-free immersion
\Phi

can be produced when either
m>3
or when

m=3
and the map

\displaystyle x\mapsto(\mathbf{t}\times\vec{n}_{0},\mathbf{t},\vec{n}_{0})(x),\quad x\in S^{1},

defines a non-nullhomotopic loop in
SO(3)
.

Note that, when
k=\alpha=1
, or when
k\geq 2
,
then
\Phi
in the above lemma satisfies the
assumptions of lemma
1.6

and can thus be conformally re-parametrised.

We treat the case
m=3
;
when
m\geq 3

see the final remark
A.4
.
For the elementary concepts of algebraic topology here mentioned
we refer the reader to
[
37
,
25
,
26
]
.

Any (non-branched) immersion
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

naturally defines a map into the space
invertible of matrices with positive determinant,
E=E_{\Phi}:\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}}\to GL^{+}(3,\mathbb{R})
,
by

\displaystyle E(x)=(\partial_{1}\Phi(x),\partial_{2}\Phi(x),\vec{n}_{\Phi}(x)),\quad x\in\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}},

where
n_{\Phi}
denotes the Gauss map of
\Phi
.
The classical Gram-Schmidt algorithm
gives the existence of a deformation retraction of
GL^{+}(3,\mathbb{R})
to
the 3-dimensional special orthogonal group
SO(3)
, and in particular the map
E
is homotopic in
GL^{+}(3,\mathbb{R})

to the coordinate frame map

\displaystyle e(x)=e_{\Phi}(x)=(e_{1}(x),e_{2}(x),e_{3}(x)),\quad x\in\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}},

where

\displaystyle e_{1}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{1}\Phi(x)}{|\partial_{1}\Phi(x)|},

\displaystyle e_{2}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{2}\Phi(x)}{|\partial_{2}\Phi(x)|}-\bigg\langle\frac{\partial_{2}\Phi(x)}{|\partial_{2}\Phi(x)|},e_{1}(x)\bigg\rangle e_{1}(x),

\displaystyle e_{3}(x)

\displaystyle=e_{1}\times e_{2}(x)=\vec{n}_{\Phi}(x).

We can similarly define the
polar frame map

defined by means of polar coordinates

x=r\mathrm{e}^{i\theta}
in
\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}}\setminus\{0\}
as

p(x)=(p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x),p_{3}(x))
,
where

\displaystyle p(r\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})=(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})(r\mathrm{e}^{i\theta})\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&0\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

We recall that the fundamental group of
SO(3)
consists precisely
of two components:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(SO(3))=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

the non-trivial class being represented for instance
by the family realising a complete rotation
around the
z
-axis:

\displaystyle R(\theta,\hat{z})=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&0\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\theta\in[0,2\pi].

(A.1)

Recall moreover that, being
SO(3)
a topological group,
the matrix product operation is compatible with the one of
\pi_{1}(SO(3))
.
Since the restriction of the coordinate frame map
e
to
S^{1}=\partial B_{1}(0)
defines a nullohomotopic loop
in
SO(3)
, the homotopy being induced by the immersion:

\displaystyle e_{t}(x)=e\left(tx\right),\quad x\in\partial B_{1}(0),\,t\in[0,1].

the polar frame defines then a non-contractible loop in
SO(3)
.
This argument implies that, given an immersed curve
\gamma:S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

and a unit-normal vector field
\vec{n}_{0}:S^{1}\to S^{2}
along
\gamma
,
a necessary condition for the existence of an immersion
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

bounding
\gamma
and so that
\vec{n}_{\Phi}=\vec{n}_{0}
on
\partial B_{1}(0)

is that

\displaystyle x\mapsto(\mathbf{t}\times\vec{n}_{0},\mathbf{t},\vec{n}_{0})(x),\quad x\in S^{1},

is
not
a nullhomotopic loop in
SO(3)
.
Examples of couples
(\gamma,n_{0})
that do not satisfy this condition are
easy to produce.

Example A.2

(Dirac Belt)
.

Let
\gamma:[0,2\pi]\to\mathbb{R}
be the unit circle:

\displaystyle\gamma(\theta)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta,0).

We consider a rotation of angle
\theta
around the
tangent vector of
\gamma
, namely

\mathbf{t}(\theta)=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta,0)
.
Its matrix is given by

\displaystyle R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))=B^{T}(\theta)R(\theta,\hat{z})B(\theta),

where

\displaystyle B(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
\cos\theta&\sin\theta&0\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\end{pmatrix},

hence we consider the polar frame map given by

\displaystyle p(\theta)=\big(R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))\hat{z}\times\mathbf{t}(\theta),\mathbf{t}(\theta),R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))\hat{z}\big),

where
\hat{z}=(0,0,1)
is the
z
-versor,
which corresponds to
rotating the polar frame map of the standard
unit disk:

\displaystyle p(\theta)=R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&0\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Using the compatibility of the product operations between
the
SO(3)
and
\pi_{1}(SO(3))
,
we see that

\displaystyle[p(\theta)]

\displaystyle=[R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))]+[R(\theta,\hat{z})]

\displaystyle=[R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))]+1.

To prove that also
R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))

belongs to the non-trivial class of
\pi_{1}(SO(3))
,
we can use its quaternion representation:

\displaystyle R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))

\displaystyle=(\cos(\theta/2),\sin(\theta/2)\,\mathbf{t}(\theta))

\displaystyle=\cos(\theta/2)-\sin(\theta/2)(-\sin\theta\mathbf{i}+\cos\theta\mathbf{j}).

With this representation, since there holds

\displaystyle R(0,\mathbf{t}(0))=1\quad\text{and}\quad R(2\pi,\mathbf{t}(2\pi))=-1,

the lift of
R(\theta,\mathbf{t}(\theta))

to the universal cover
S^{3}
is not a closed loop,
and this means that the base loop is not nullhomotopic.
We then conclude that

\displaystyle[p(\theta)]=1+1=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}.

This example demonstrates also that a couple
(\gamma,\vec{n}_{0})

needs not to bound an immersion of
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

not even if
\gamma
is planar and injective.
We now want to prove that the only additional requirement for
\Phi

to exist is to have a branch point.
The key step to prove it,
obtained through an elementary application of the
so-called
h
-principle

[
28
,
36
]
,
is the following lemma.
In what follows, let us denote, for
0\leq r\leq R<\infty
,

\displaystyle A[R,r]=\overline{B_{R}(0)}\setminus B_{r}(0)

the annulus of radii
R
and
r
centered at 0.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}:S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

be regular, closed curves of class
C^{k,\alpha}
for

k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
whose unit tangent vectors we denote
by
\mathbf{t}_{1}
and
\mathbf{t}_{2}
,
and let
\vec{n}_{0},\vec{n}_{1}:S^{1}\to S^{2}
be unit normal vector fields along

\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
respectively of class
C^{k,\alpha}
.
There exists a regular, immersed strip of class
C^{k,\alpha}

\displaystyle\Phi:A[2,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3},

satisfying

\displaystyle\Phi|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\gamma_{1},\quad\vec{n}_{\Phi}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\vec{n}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\gamma_{2},\quad\vec{n}_{\Phi}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\vec{n}_{2}

(A.2)

if and only if the maps

\displaystyle p_{1}(x)=(\mathbf{t}_{1}\times\vec{n}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{1},\vec{n}_{1})(x)\quad\text{and}\quad p_{2}(x)=(\mathbf{t}_{2}\times\vec{n}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{2},\vec{n}_{2})(x),\quad x\in S^{1},

are homotopic in
SO(3)
.

Proof of Lemma
A.3
.

The necessity of the condition is clear, we prove the sufficiency.

Step 1.

Set, for
\delta>0
,
K_{\delta}=S^{1}\times(-\delta,\delta)^{2}

and define the following maps for
i=1,2
:

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\xi,u,v)

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}(\xi)+u\,(\mathbf{t}_{i}\times\vec{n}_{i})(\xi)+v\,\vec{n}_{i}(\xi),(\xi,u,v)\in\overline{K_{\delta}}.

If
\delta
is chosen small enough,
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}

define regular immersions (i.e.
the Jacobian matrix
D\phi(x)
has rank 3 for every
x\in\overline{K}_{\delta}
)
of class
C^{k,\alpha}
.
Since
S^{1}
and
SO(3)
are strong deformation retracts of
\overline{K_{\delta}}

and
GL^{+}(3,\mathbb{R})
respectively,
an homotopy between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
in
SO(4)
induce an homotopy in
GL^{+}(3,\mathbb{R})

between
D\phi_{1}
and
D\phi_{2}
.
Let
(x,t)\mapsto m(x,t)
,
(x,t)\in\overline{K_{\delta}}\times[0,1]
be such an homotopy.

Step 2.

Let
J^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the 1-jet space of maps from
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to itself
(see
[
28
, Chapter 1]
) and let us consider the (local) section

\displaystyle F:S^{1}\times\left[-\delta,\frac{5}{2}\delta\right]\times[-\delta,\delta]\to J^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad x\mapsto(x,\phi(x),M(x)),

where

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\phi(x)=\phi(\xi,u,v)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\phi_{1}(\xi,u,v)&\text{if }u\in\left[-\delta,\frac{\delta}{2}\right],\\
\frac{2}{\delta}(\delta-u)\phi_{1}(\xi,u,v)+\frac{2}{\delta}\left(\frac{\delta}{2}-u\right)\phi_{2}\left(\xi,u-\frac{3}{2}\delta,v\right)&\text{if }u\in\left[-\frac{\delta}{2},\delta\right],\\
\phi_{2}\left(\xi,u-\frac{3}{2}\delta,v\right)&\text{if }u\in\left[\delta,\frac{5}{2}\delta\right],\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}M(x)=M(\xi,u,v)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}D\phi_{1}(\xi,u,v)&\text{if }u\in\left[-\delta,\frac{\delta}{2}\right],\\
m\left(\xi,u-3\left(u-\frac{\delta}{2}\right),v,\frac{2}{\delta}\left(1-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right)&\text{if }u\in\left[-\frac{\delta}{2},\delta\right],\\
D\phi_{2}\left(\xi,u-\frac{3}{2}\delta,v\right)&\text{if }u\in\left[\delta,\frac{5}{2}\delta\right],\end{cases}

Performing a normalisation of the parameters, we obtain a section

G:\overline{K_{1/2}}=S^{1}\times[-1,1]\times[-1,1]\to J^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})

which is holonomic in the set

S^{1}\times[-1,-1/4]\times[-1,1]\cup S^{1}\times[1/4,1]\times[-1,1]
.

Step 3.

By the relative version of the Holonomic Approximation theorem
(
[
28
, Theorems 3.1.1, 3.2.1]
)
with

\displaystyle A=S^{1}\times\left[-\frac{3}{4},\frac{3}{4}\right]\times\{0\},\quad B=S^{1}\times\left\{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right\}\times\{0\},

we may obtain, for every
\varepsilon_{1}>0
, a diffeomorphism
h:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

with
\|h-\Id_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\varepsilon_{1}

and satisfying
h\equiv\Id_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
on a open neighbourhood
U
of
B
,
a holonomic section
\tilde{G}:V\to J^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
V\supseteq U
is an
open neighbourhood of
h(A)
, satisfying
\tilde{G}\equiv G
on
U

and
\|\tilde{F}-F\|_{C^{0}(V)}\leq\varepsilon_{1}
.
By choosing
\varepsilon_{1}
small enough,
\tilde{G}
is then the
1-jet extension of an immersion coinciding
with the one of
\phi_{0}
in a open neighbourhood of
S^{1}\times\{-1/2\}\times\{0\}

and with the one of
\phi_{1}
in a open neighbourhood of
S^{1}\times\{1/2\}\times\{0\}
;
in particular it is of class
C^{k,\alpha}
in such neighbourhoods.

Possibly reducing
U
and
V
,
the existence of a diffeomorphism
g:K_{1/2}\to V
that shrinks
K_{1/2}
into
V
wile keeping
U
fixed is ensured.
If we consider the restriction of holonomic section
H=G\circ g:\overline{K_{1/2}}\to J^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathbb{R}^{3})
to
S^{1}\times[-1/2,1/2]\times\{0\}
,
and denote by
\Psi:S^{1}\times[-1/2,1/2]\simeq A[2,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
the base
map,
we have that
\Psi
is realises a regular immersion of class
C^{1}

which, in a small neighdourhood of the
boundary, containing, say,
S^{1}\times[1/2+\varepsilon_{2},1/2-\varepsilon_{2}]

for some small
\varepsilon_{2}>0
,
and of class
C^{k,\alpha}
and satisfies the desired boundary prescriptions
(
A.2
).

Step 4.

To ensure the global
C^{k,\alpha}
regularity,
we us use a localised mollification
for
\Psi
:

\displaystyle\Phi(x)=\Psi*\rho_{\varepsilon(x)}(x)\quad x\in S^{1}\times\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right],

where
\rho
is the standard mollification kernel and

\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon_{3}\chi(x)
, where
\chi
is smooth
cut-off function identically 1 on
S^{1}\times[-1/2+\varepsilon_{3},1/2-\varepsilon_{3}]

and compactly supported on
S^{1}\times(-1/2,1/2)

and
\varepsilon_{3}>0
has be chosen so small that
\Phi
has maximum rank.
We conclude that the map
\Phi
thus defined is the one we have been looking for.
∎

From this lemma we can prove lemma
A.1
,
in the case
m=3
, that is we can construct,
given any couple curve-normal vector field
(\gamma,n_{0})
of
class
C^{k,\alpha}
, a possibly branched immersion
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

of class
C^{k,\alpha}
assuming such data at the boundary.

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

We consider the the loop

p(x)=(\mathbf{t}\times n_{0},\mathbf{t},n_{0})(x)
in
SO(3)

induced by
Gamma
and
\vec{n}_{0}
.
If it is not nullhomotopic, we can connect, in a
C^{1}
way, the couple
(\gamma,n_{0})

and the flat immersion of the disk
z\mapsto(z,0)
by means of a regular strip of
class
C^{k,\alpha}
.
If it is nullhomotopic instead we can do the same with the
branched immersion
z\mapsto(z^{2},0)
.
if necessary, we smooth out the immersion near the junction
as done in Step 4 of the proof of lemma
A.3
;
a final reparametrization of gives then the immersion
\Phi:\overline{B_{1}(0)^{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}

we have been looking for.

For the last statement,
it is enough to note that when
k=\alpha=1
or
k\geq 2
, this
\Phi

satisfies all the assumptions of lemma
1.6
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark A.4

(Higher codimension case)
.

For general
m\geq 3
, a regular curve

\gamma:S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
(m-2)
-unit normal vector field

\vec{n}_{0}:S^{1}\to\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
along
\gamma

uniquely determine a loop into the set of couples of ortho-normal
vectors or
\mathbb{R}^{m}
:

\displaystyle x\mapsto(\star(\mathbf{t}\wedge\vec{n}_{0}),\mathbf{t})(x)\quad x\in S^{1},

that is to say, into the Stiefel manifold
V_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

(see e.g.
[
37
]
), which
for the case
m=3
we could identify with
SO(3)
.
As it is well-known,
\pi_{1}(V_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))=0
for
m>3

and hence, the higher-dimensional version of lemma
A.3

basically says that a regular strip bounding any two couples
(\gamma_{1},\vec{n}_{1})

and
(\gamma_{2},\vec{n}_{2})
can always be constructed.
As a consequence, with the aid of the Holonomic approximation,
in a similar fashion as the one just described,
we may always find a regular immersion bounding
\gamma
and
\vec{n}_{0}
.
This is perhaps not so surprising, as higher codimension gives us
more freedom.

A.2
Results on Integrability by Compensation

Theorem A.5

(Wente’s Inequality,
[
66
,
67
,
10
]
)
.

Let
a,b\in W^{1,2}(D)
and let
u\in W^{1,1}_{0}(B_{1}(0))
be the solution
to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla b\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
u&=0&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0).\end{aligned}\right.

Then
u\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
and there is a constant
C>0
independent of
u,a
and
b

so that

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}+\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\|\nabla b\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}.

(A.3)

For the following lemma we use a result obtained in
[
16
]
.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
a,b\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
be so that their traces
a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)},b|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}

belong to
W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))
for some
p>1
.
Then there exist a constant
C>0
independent of
u,a
and
b
and constant
C(p)>0
depending only on
p
so that
every solution
u\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
of the problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla b\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=\partial_{\tau}a\,b&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

(A.4)

belongs to
C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
and satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}+\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|u-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\|b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\left(\left\|\partial_{\tau}a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\right\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|b|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

Remark A.7
.

Any weak solution of (
A.4
)
is naturally in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
, since it is found by considering
the minimisation of the the functional

\displaystyle w\mapsto\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla w-b\nabla^{\perp}a|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x,

among all the functions in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
with a fixed average.
Any two solutions of (
A.4
) differ by a constant.

Proof of Lemma
A.6
.

Step 1.

We establish first the
L^{\infty}
estimate for the analogous problem on the upper half-plane

\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}=\{(x^{1},x^{2}):x^{2}>0\}
for
a,b
smooth and compactly supported
in some ball
B_{R}(0)
of fixed radius
R>0
,
namely

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla b\rangle&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=\partial_{\tau}a\,b&&\text{on }\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.\end{aligned}\right.

(A.6)

Because of the “compatible” boundary condition (using a terminology from

[
16
]
),
the only solution of (
A.6
)
belonging to
W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})

is given by the representation formula

\displaystyle u(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\langle\nabla_{y}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(x,y),b(y)\nabla^{\perp}a(y)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},

(A.7)

where
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}
is the Green function for the Neumann
problem in the half-plane given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(x,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\log|x-y|+\log|\overline{x}-y|\right),\quad(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},

(here
\overline{x}
denotes the complex conjugate of
x
,
that is if
x=(x^{1},x^{2})
then
\overline{x}=(x^{1},-x^{2})
).
Note that such formula implies that
u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}})

8
8

8

this need
not
to be the case,
not even for smooth
a
and
b
, for different Neumann
(for example homogeneous) boundary conditions.
.
As proved in
[
16
, §3.1]
, the trace of the solution

u
of (
A.6
) given
by (
A.7
) can be written as

\displaystyle u(x^{1},0)=A(a,b)(x^{1})+B(a,b)(x^{2}),\quad x^{1}\in\mathbb{R}\simeq\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+},

where the function
A(a,b)
satisfies

\displaystyle\|A(a,b)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\|\nabla b\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},

(A.8)

and the function
B(a,b)
is given by

\displaystyle B(a,b)(x^{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\pv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}\left(a(x^{1}+t,0)+a(x^{1}-t,0)\right)\left(b(x^{1}-t,0)-b(x^{1}+t,0)\right)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\pv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}a(x^{1}+t,0)b(x^{1}-t,0)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\pi}\pv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}a(x^{1}-t,0)b(x^{1}-t,0)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(A.9)

We recognise that the second term in the above expression is
the Hilbert transform
H(ab)
of
ab
(see e.g.
[
62
]
or
[
35
, §5.1]
).
We recall that the Hilbert transform
H
is a tempered distribution
which maps
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
into itself for
1<p<\infty
;
moreover it is a convolution-type distribution and consequently
it commutes with derivatives.
This implies that, for a constant
C(p)>0
depending only on
p
,
there holds

\displaystyle\|H(f)\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C(p)\|f\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})},\quad\forall\,f\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}).

On the other hand, by the Sobolev embedding we have

W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow C^{0,\gamma}(\mathbb{R})

continuously with
\gamma=1-1/p
.
This implies in particular that
W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})
is an algebra,
that is

\displaystyle\|f\,g\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C(p)\|f\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\|g\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\quad\forall\,f,g\in W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R}),

where
C(p)>0
is a constant depending only on
p
.
Using these facts we may estimate the second term in
expression (
A.9
)
uniformly in
x^{1}
as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\pi}\pv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}a(x^{1}-t,0)b(x^{1}-t,0)\,\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq\|H(ab)\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C(p)\|a\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\|b\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}.

(A.10)

The first term in expression (
A.9
)
may be similarly estimated as follows:
fix
x^{1}
and define the function
h_{x^{1}}(t)=a(x^{1}+t,0)b(x^{1}-t,0)
.
We then have:

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{\pi}\pv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{t}a(x^{1}+t,0)b(x^{1}-t,0)\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg|

\displaystyle=|H(h_{x^{1}})(0)|

(A.11)

\displaystyle\leq\|H(h_{x^{1}})\|_{C^{0,\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\|h_{x^{1}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\|a(x^{1}+\cdot)\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\|a(x^{1}-\cdot)\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=C(p)\|a\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}\|b\|_{W^{1,p}(\mathbb{R})}.

We may then join estimates (
A.8
), (
A.10
)
and (
A.11
) to deduce the following bound on the trace of
u
:

\displaystyle\|u|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\|\nabla b\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\left(\|a|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\|b|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}\right).

Step 2.

We now come to the problem on the disk, that is, we consider a solution

u
of the problem (
A.4
).
By an approximation argument we may suppose that
a,b\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})

and, as in step 1, because of the “compatible” boundary condition, the solution is
represented by

\displaystyle u(x)=-\int_{B_{1}(0)}\langle\nabla_{y}\mathcal{G}(x,y),b(y)\nabla^{\perp}a(y)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}y\quad x\in B_{1}(0),

where
\mathcal{G}
is the Green function for the Neumann problem
(see (
2.3
)), and in particular we have

u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
.
Let us fix a function
\chi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})

so that
\chi=1
in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\cap\{x^{1}>0\}
and
whose support is contained in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\cap\{x^{1}\geq-1/2\}
.
We then write
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
, where

9
9

9

note that
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are determined up to a constant.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{1}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla(\chi b)\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{1}&=[\partial_{\tau}a]\,(\chi b)&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{2}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a),\nabla((1-\chi)b)\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{2}&=[\partial_{\tau}a]\,((1-\chi)b)&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0).\end{aligned}\right.

If we fix
a function
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
so that

\psi=1
in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\cap\{x^{1}\geq-1/2\}
and whose support
is contained in
\overline{B_{1}(0)}\cap\{x^{1}\geq-3/4\}
,
we may write

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{1}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}(\psi a),\nabla(\chi b)\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{1}&=[\partial_{\tau}(\psi a)]\,(\chi b)&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

If
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\overset{\sim}{\to}B_{1}(0)
denotes the fractional linear transformation
sending
0
to
1
,
i
to
0
and
-1
to
\infty
,
given in complex coordinates by
\varphi(z)=-(z-i)/(z+i)
,

(\psi a)\circ\varphi
and
(\chi b)\circ\varphi
have compact support
and with estimates

\displaystyle\|\nabla((\psi a)\circ\varphi)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|a\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))},

\displaystyle\|((\psi a)\circ\varphi)|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},\leq C(p)\|a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))},

\displaystyle\|\nabla((\chi b)\circ\varphi)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))},

\displaystyle\|((\chi a)\circ\varphi)|_{\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})},\leq C(p)\|a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}.

The invariance of Dirichlet energy and of the problem (
A.4
)
implies that, if
we compose
u_{1}

\psi a
and
\chi b
with
\varphi
,
then there must be a constant
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
so that

\displaystyle u_{1}\circ\varphi(x)-c_{1}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\langle\nabla_{y}\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}(x,y),((\chi b)\circ\varphi)(y)\nabla^{\perp}((\psi a)\circ\varphi)\rangle(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}.

We then deduce from estimate (
A.12
),
that the trace
u_{1}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}
can be estimated as

\displaystyle\|u_{1}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}-c_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle=\|(u_{1}\circ\varphi)|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}-c_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+})}

(A.13)

\displaystyle\leq C\|a\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}\|b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\left(\|a|_{\partial_{B_{1}(0)}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|b|_{\partial_{B_{1}(0)}}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

In the same way,
by working with
1-\chi
,
\psi(-z)
and
\varphi(-z)

ins place of
\chi
,
\psi
and
\phi
respectively,
we obtain an analogous estimate for
u_{2}|_{\partial_{B_{1}(0)}}-c_{2}
for some
c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since the problem (
A.4
)
is invariant under translations with respect to
a
, that is, for any
c\in\mathbb{R}

we have

\langle\nabla^{\perp}(a-c),\nabla b\rangle=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla b\rangle
in
B_{1}(0)
and

\partial_{\tau}(a-c)\,b=\partial_{\tau}a\,b
on
\partial B_{1}(0)
,
we conclude that

\displaystyle\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|u|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}-c\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|a-c\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}\|b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}

(A.14)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\left(\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}-c\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|b|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

holds. By the maximum principle for harmonic functions,
Poincaré’s inequality
and Wente’s inequality (
A.3
) we
finally deduce the the validity of
the
L^{\infty}
-part of estimate
of
(
A.5
).

The estimate on the Dirichlet energy of
u
follows
integrating by parts
(recall that, by approximation, we are working with

u\in C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})
), namely:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{1}(0)}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=-\int_{B_{1}(0)}(u-c)\Delta u\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{B_{1}(0)}(u-c)\partial_{\nu}u\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}\quad\text{for every}c\in\mathbb{R},

hence estimating the terms on the right-hand-side with
Hölder and Sobolev inequalities:

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{B_{1}(0)}(u-c)\Delta u\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|u-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\|\nabla b\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))},

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}(u-c)\partial_{\nu}u\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\|u-c\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|\partial_{\tau}a\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|b\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\leq C(p)\|u-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}\|\partial_{\tau}a\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|b\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}.

and using finally the
L^{\infty}
-estimate above obtained.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

With a standard argument, we may localise the
above result as follows.

Lemma A.8
.

Let
f\in L^{1}(B_{1}(0))
,
g\in L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))
,

a_{1},\ldots a_{N}
be points in
B_{1}(0)

and
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}
be real numbers
satisfying
\int_{B_{1}(0)}f\,\mathrm{d}x+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=-\int_{\partial_{B_{1}(0)}}g\,\mathrm{d}\sigma
.
Let
u\in W^{1,1}(B_{1}(0))
be a weak solution to the problem

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u&=f+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u&=g&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0).\end{aligned}\right.

(A.15)

Assume that, for a given
x_{0}\in\partial B_{1}(0)
and
0<r<1
,

B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
contains none of the
a_{i}
’s and there holds

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla b\rangle\text{ in }B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}a\,b\text{ on }\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

for some
a,b\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))
so that the traces on
\partial B_{1}(0)

a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)},b|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}

belong to
W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))
for some
p>1
.
Then
u\in C^{0}(\overline{B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0})})\cap W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))
and there exists
constants
C(r)>0

C(r,p)>0
so that

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}\inf_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\|u-c\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}

(A.16)

\displaystyle\leq C(r)\bigg(\|f\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\|g\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\alpha_{i}|\bigg)+C(r)\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\|b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(r,p)\|\partial_{\tau}a|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\|b|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}.

Proof of Lemma
A.8
.

We let
\chi
be a function in
C^{\infty}(\overline{B_{1}(0)})

so that
\chi=1
in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{3r/4}(x_{0})

and whose support is contained in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{7r/8}(x_{0})
,
and we let
\tilde{a}
be an extension of
a
to
B_{1}(0)
,
obtained through a suitable Moebius tranformation of
B_{1}(0)

so that
\|\nabla\tilde{a}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla a\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}

and

\|\partial_{\tau}\tilde{a}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq C(p)\|\partial_{\tau}a\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}

for constants
C,C(p)>0
.
Up to a constant, we may write
u=u_{1}+u_{2}
, with

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{1}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\tilde{a},\nabla(\chi b)\rangle&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{1}&=[\partial_{\tau}\tilde{a}]\,(\chi b)&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} -\Delta u_{2}&=\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla((1-\chi)b)\rangle+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\delta_{a_{i}}&&\text{in }B_{1}(0),\\
\partial_{\nu}u_{2}&=[\partial_{\tau}a]\,((1-\chi)b)&&\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{aligned}\right.

with the convention that

\displaystyle\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla((1-\chi)b)\rangle=f\text{ in }B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{7r/8}(x_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad[\partial_{\tau}a]\,((1-\chi)b)=g\text{ on }\partial B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{7r/8}(x_{0}).

From lemma
A.6
,
we deduce that for some constant
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
there holds

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\|u_{1}-c_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}{+\|\nabla u_{1}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}}

(A.17)

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla\tilde{a}\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}\|\chi b\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))}

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}+C(p)\left(\left\|\partial_{\tau}\tilde{a}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\right\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\|(\chi b)|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\|_{W^{1,p}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

To estimate
u_{2}
, we use the representation formula:

\displaystyle u_{2}(x)-\overline{u}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla((1-\chi)b)\rangle(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\phantom{{}+{}}+\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\mathcal{G}(x,y)\langle\partial_{\tau}a,(1-\chi)b\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}(y)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{G}(x,a_{i}),

since none of the
a_{i}
’s is in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})

and
1-\chi
vanishes
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{3r/4}(x_{0})
,
we may estimate on
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0})
:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}\leq{}}\|u_{2}-\overline{u}_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(x_{0}))}

(A.18)

\displaystyle\leq C(r)\bigg(\|\langle\nabla^{\perp}a,\nabla((1-\chi)b)\rangle\|_{L^{1}(B_{1}(0))}+\|[\partial_{\tau}a](1-\chi)b\rangle\|_{L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\alpha_{i}|\bigg).

Since
\chi
can always be chosen so that

\|\nabla\chi\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C/r
,
by joining estimates (
A.17
) and
(
A.18
) we reach the conclusion.
∎

A.3
Facts About Moving Frames

A
moving frame
, or simply a
frame
, from a domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}

is a map
\vec{f}=(\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
so that
\langle\vec{f}_{i},\vec{f}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
.
If
\vec{f}
and
\vec{g}
are two frames from
\Omega
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, a function
\phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

defines a way to pass from
\vec{f}
to
\vec{g}
, i.e. a
gauge transformation
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \vec{g}_{1}(x)&=\vec{f}_{1}(x)\cos\phi(x)+\vec{f}_{2}(x)\sin\phi(x),\\
\vec{g}_{2}(x)&=-\vec{f}_{1}(x)\sin\phi(x)+\vec{f}_{2}(x)\cos\phi(x),\\
\end{aligned}\right.

which can be written using the complex notation as

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{1}+i\vec{g}_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\phi}(\vec{f}_{1}+i\vec{f}_{2})\quad\text{in }\Omega.

Differentiating this relation, we deduce the following
Change of gauge formula
:

\displaystyle\langle\nabla\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2}\rangle=\nabla\phi+\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle\quad\text{in }\Omega.

(A.19)

For a map
\vec{n}:B_{1}(0)\to\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

expressed as
\vec{n}=\vec{n}_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\vec{n}_{m-2}

for some
(m-2)
-tuple of ortho-normal sections:
\langle\vec{n}_{i},\vec{n}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
,
we say that the frame
(\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2})

is a lift for
\vec{n}
if:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{f}_{1}\wedge\vec{f}_{2})\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0),

where

\star
denotes the Euclidean Hodge operator in
\mathbb{R}^{m}

transforming
2
vectors into
m-2
vectors and vice-versa
(see e.g.
[
8
]
).
By orthonormality, there holds:

\displaystyle\nabla\vec{f}_{1}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle\vec{f}_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{n}_{i}\rangle\vec{n}_{i}=\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle\vec{f}_{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}\langle\vec{f}_{1},\nabla\vec{n}_{i}\rangle\vec{n}_{i},

\displaystyle\nabla\vec{f}_{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{2},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle\vec{f}_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{2},\vec{n}_{i}\rangle\vec{n}_{i}=\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{2},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle\vec{f}_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{m-2}\langle\vec{f}_{2},\nabla\vec{n}_{i}\rangle\vec{n}_{i},

\displaystyle\nabla\vec{n}_{i}

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla\vec{n}_{i},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle\vec{f}_{1}+\langle\nabla\vec{n}_{i},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle\vec{f}_{2}\quad(i=1,\ldots,m-2),

in particular:

\displaystyle|\nabla\vec{f}_{1}|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{f}_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2|\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}.

(A.20)

When
\vec{n}=\vec{n}_{\Phi}
is the Gauss map of an immersion
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

and
(\vec{f},\vec{f}_{2})
is a positively oriented ortho-normal basis of the tangent space.
When
\vec{f}
and
\vec{n}
correspond to a orthonormal frame and Gauss map of an immersion
\Phi
,
an elementary computation reveals that

\displaystyle K_{\Phi}\,\mathrm{d}vol_{\Phi}=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{f}_{1},\vec{n}\rangle\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{2},\vec{n}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x=\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{f}_{1},\nabla\vec{f}_{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}x,

(A.21)

where
K_{\Phi}
is the Gauss curvature of
\Phi
and
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Phi}
its area element.
This equation has two important consequences:
first, it reveals that the the left-hand-side is a sum of Jacobians.
Second, if
\vec{n}
is sufficiently regular (namely, that it admits a lifting frame
\vec{f}
:
see lemmas
A.9
and
A.10
below)
then it has a meaning also when
\Phi
is singular, for example at a branch point,
when a tangent plane is not defined.
Finally, it is useful to note that

\displaystyle|\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{f}_{1},\nabla\vec{f}_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(|\langle\nabla^{\perp}\vec{f}_{1},\vec{n}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{2},\vec{n}\rangle|^{2}\right)=\frac{|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}}{2}.

(A.22)

Recall Hélein’s lifting lemma
(
[
38
, Lemma 5.1.4]
, see also
[
45
]
).

Lemma A.9
.

There is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that, for every
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
with the following property.
If a map

\vec{n}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq\varepsilon

for some
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
then there exist an orthonormal frame

\vec{f}=(\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2})\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})

which is a positive orthonormal basis for
\vec{n}
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{f}_{1}\wedge\vec{f}_{2})\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0),

that satisfies the following Coulomb condition:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\divop(\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle)&=0\text{ in }B_{1}(0),\\
\langle\partial_{\nu}\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle&=0\text{ on }\partial B_{1}(0),\end{cases}

(A.23)

and whose energy is controlled as follows:

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{f}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}.

(A.24)

The proof consists on a minimizing procedure and
the
L^{2}
-bound relies on Wente’s lemma.
The following variant concerns the existence of
a energy-controlled lift with a prescribed boundary value.

Lemma A.10
.

There is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that, for every
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
with the following property.
For any map
\vec{n}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

and any ortho-normal frame

\vec{e}=(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})\in H^{1/2}(\partial B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})

lifting
\vec{n}
:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{e}_{1}\wedge\vec{e}_{2})\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),

and satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq\varepsilon,

(A.25)

there exists an ortho-normal frame

\vec{g}=(\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2})\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
lifting
\vec{n}
:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{g}_{1}\wedge\vec{g}_{2})\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0),

(A.26)

whose trace on
\partial_{B_{1}(0)}
coincides with
\vec{e}
, satisfying
the Coulomb condition

\displaystyle\divop\left(\langle\nabla\vec{g_{1}},g_{2}\rangle\right)=0\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0),

(A.27)

and the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{g}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

(A.28)

Proof of Lemma
A.10
.

Let us start by fixing
\varepsilon_{0}<2\pi
, so that
by lemma (
A.9
) we deduce the existence of a
Coulomb frame
\vec{f}
on
B_{1}(0)
satisfying

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}.

(A.29)

We now want to identify the angle
\alpha_{0}:\partial B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}
which rotates
f

to
\vec{e}
, implicitly defined in complex notation by

\vec{e}_{1}+i\vec{e}_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\alpha}(\vec{f}_{1}+i\vec{f}_{2})
.
To this aim, let us define the
S^{1}
-valued function:

\displaystyle u=\langle\vec{e}_{1},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle-i\langle\vec{e}_{2},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),

and note that it belongs to
W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0),S^{1})
,
satisfying

10
10

10

Recall that if
\Omega
is a domain of
\mathbb{R}
or of
S^{1}
, the space

(W^{1/2,2}\cap L^{\infty})(\Omega)

is an algebra with:

\displaystyle[ab]_{W^{1/2,2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq 2(\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}[b]_{W^{1/2,2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|b\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}[a]_{W^{1/2,2}(\Omega)}^{2}).

because of (
A.25
)
and (
A.29
) the estimate

\displaystyle[u]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial_{B_{1}(0)})}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left([\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}+[\vec{f}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right)\leq C\varepsilon,

(A.30)

for some constant
C>0
.
By choosing
\varepsilon_{0}>0
sufficiently small,
we may then invoke theorem
[
55
, Theorem 1]

and deduce the existence of a function
U\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),S^{1})

whose trace on
\partial_{B_{1}(0)}
coincides with

u

and satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C[u]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial_{B_{1}(0)})},

(A.31)

for some
C>0
.
For such
U
, we may now deduce the existence
of a lift
\alpha\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0))
(see e.g.
[
9
, Theorem 3]
),
that is,

\displaystyle U(x)=\mathrm{e}^{i\alpha(x)}\quad x\in B_{1}(0),

satisfying the point-wise almost everywhere estimate
|\nabla U|=|\nabla\alpha|
.
if
\widetilde{\alpha}
denotes the harmonic extension of
the trace of
\alpha
on
\partial B_{1}(0)
,
the Dirichlet principle together with the inequalities
(
A.25
) and (
A.30
)
imply

\displaystyle\|\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\right).

(A.32)

Finally, we set:

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{1}+i\vec{g}_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\widetilde{\alpha}}(\vec{f}_{1}+i\vec{f}_{2})\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0).

By construction, the frame
\vec{g}

has trace equal to
\vec{e}
on
\partial B_{1}(0)
,
and satisfies conditions (
A.26
), (
A.27
)
and from formula (
A.20
), we see that almost everywhere in

B_{1}(0)

the relation

\displaystyle|\nabla\vec{g}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2|\langle\nabla\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2}\rangle|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2|\langle\nabla\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2}\rangle+\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4|\nabla\vec{f}|^{2}+|\nabla\widetilde{\alpha}|^{2}

holds,
from which we deduce, thanks to inequalities (
A.29
)
and (
A.32
),
the validity of (
A.31
).
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A localised version of the above lemma is as follows.

Lemma A.11
.

There is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
so that, for every
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,
a constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
with the following property exists.
Let
x_{0}\in\partial B_{1}(0)
and
0<r<1
be fixed.
For any map
\vec{n}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

and any ortho-normal frame

\vec{e}=(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})\in W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})

lifting
\vec{n}
:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{e}_{1}\wedge\vec{e}_{2})\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

and satisfying the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq\varepsilon,

there exists an ortho-normal frame

\vec{g}=(\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2})\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})

lifting
\vec{n}
:

\displaystyle\vec{n}=\star(\vec{g}_{1}\wedge\vec{g}_{2})\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

whose trace on
\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
coincides with
\vec{e}
, satisfying
the Coulomb condition

\displaystyle\divop\left(\langle\nabla\vec{g_{1}},g_{2}\rangle\right)=0\quad\text{in }B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}),

and the estimate

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{g}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq C\left(\|\nabla\vec{n}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}+[\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}\right).

The proof makes use of the following elementary result.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
0<\theta_{0}<\pi
be fixed and
f:(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})\to\mathbb{C}

be a
W^{1/2,2}
-function.
Let
F
be its extension to
S^{1}\simeq(-\pi,\pi]/\sim

by even reflection defined by:

\displaystyle F(x)=\begin{cases}f(x)&\text{if }x\in(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}),\\
f(m(x-\sign(x)\pi))&\text{if }x\in(-\pi,\pi]\setminus(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}),\end{cases}

(A.33)

where
m=\frac{\theta_{0}}{\theta_{0}-\pi}
.
Then
F\in W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})
and there holds:

\displaystyle[F]_{W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})}\leq 2[{f}]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}.

Proof of Lemma
A.12
.

Note first of all that we may equivalently write
F=f\circ j
, where

j:[-\pi,\pi]\to[-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}]
is defined as:

\displaystyle j(x)=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }x\in(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}),\\
m(x-\sign(x)\pi)&\text{if }x\in(-\pi,\pi]\setminus(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}).\end{cases}

Using the invariance by rescaling of the
W^{1/2,2}
-seminorm and Tonelli’s theorem, we see that:

\displaystyle[F]_{W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})}^{2}=2[f]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}^{2}+2\int_{S^{1}\setminus(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\int_{(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{iy}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thinking of
j
as a diffeomorphism from
S^{1}\setminus[-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}]

to
[-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}]
with
j^{\prime}=m
, we perform a change variable in the above integral
as follows:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\int_{S^{1}\setminus(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\int_{(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{iy}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\setminus(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\int_{(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\frac{|{f}(x)-{f}(j(y))|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{iy}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|m|}\int_{(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\int_{(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})}\frac{|{f}(x)-{f}(\eta)|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{i(j^{-1}(\eta))}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

Suppose now that
\theta_{0}=\pi/2
:
we have
j^{-1}(\eta)=-\eta+\sign(\eta)\pi
and
since
x,\eta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
, there holds

|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{i(j^{-1}(\eta))}|\geq|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{i\eta}|
,
hence:

\displaystyle\int_{S^{1}\setminus(-\pi/2,\pi/2)}\int_{(-\pi/2,\pi/2)}\frac{|F(x)-F(y)|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{iy}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(-\pi/2,\pi/2)}\int_{(-\pi/2,\pi/2)}\frac{|{f}(x)-{f}(\eta)|^{2}}{|\mathrm{e}^{ix}-\mathrm{e}^{i\eta}|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\eta=[f]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\pi/2,\pi/2))}^{2},

So we conclude that:

\displaystyle[F]_{W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})}^{2}\leq 4[f]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\pi/2,\pi/2))}^{2}.

For a general
0<\theta_{0}<\pi
, we may reduce to the case
\theta_{0}=\pi/2

by using the fact that the
H^{1/2}
-seminorm is invariant with respect to
the restriction to
S^{1}
of Moebius transformation of
D
.
In our particular case, the transformation we need is:

\displaystyle M_{a}(z)=\frac{z+a}{\overline{a}z+1}\quad\text{with }a=\frac{\pi/2-\theta_{0}}{1-(\pi/2)\theta_{0}},\quad\text{for }z\in S^{1}.

In other words:

\displaystyle[F]_{W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})}=[F\circ M_{a}]_{W^{1/2,2}(S^{1})},

so we may apply the previous inequality and reach the conclusion.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark A.13
.

Lemma
A.12
holds also for domains which are conformally equivalent to
B_{1}(0)
.

Proof of Lemma
A.11
.

Without loss of generality we may assume
x_{0}=1
,
so that we have the identification
\partial_{B_{1}(0)}\cap B_{r}(x_{0})\simeq(-\theta_{0},\theta_{0})

for some
0<\theta_{0}<\pi
.

We define a map
\vec{N}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

which coincides with the given
\vec{n}
in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})
and has
globally controlled energy, as follows.
First,
define
\vec{n}^{\prime}\in\dot{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{C},\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
as the extension of
\vec{n}
to
\mathbb{C}
through even reflection:

\displaystyle\vec{n}^{\prime}(z)=\begin{cases}\vec{n}(z)&\text{if }z\in B_{1}(0),\\
\vec{n}\left(\frac{z}{|z|^{2}}\right)&\text{if }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{1}(0).\end{cases}

By the conformal invariance of the Dirichlet energy, there holds

\|\nabla\vec{n}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{C})}=2\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}

and

11
11

11

If
I(z)=z/|z|^{2}
denotes the inversion with respect to the unit circle,
then:

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{2}\setminus D)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))\subset I(D\cap B_{r}(x_{0})).

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{n}^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{r}(x_{0}))}\leq 2\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(D\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}.

(A.34)

Consider now
\vec{n}^{\prime}
as a map from
B_{r}(x_{0})
and
define
\vec{N}\in\dot{W}^{1,2}(\mathbb{C},\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

to be its extension through even reflection:

\displaystyle\vec{N}(z)=\begin{cases}\vec{n}^{\prime}(z)&\text{if }z\in B_{r}(x_{0}),\\
\vec{n}^{\prime}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{|z-x_{0}|^{2}}(z-x_{0})\right)&\text{if }z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus B_{r}(x_{0}).\end{cases}

By the conformal invariance of the Dirichlet energy
and (
A.34
), there holds

\|\nabla\vec{N}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{C})}\leq 4\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}
,
hence a fortiori:

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{N}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq 4\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}.

Consequently, by assuming
4\varepsilon_{0}<2\pi
,
we may invoke lemma

A.9
and find a Coulomb orthonormal frame

\vec{f}=(\vec{f}_{1},\vec{f}_{2})\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
lifting
\vec{N}

in
B_{1}(0)
and satisfying:

\displaystyle\|\nabla\vec{f}\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0))}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\|\nabla\vec{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0}))}.

As in the proof of lemma
A.10
,
the angle
\alpha_{0}:\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(x_{0})\to\mathbb{R}
which rotates
\vec{f}

to
\vec{e}
is implicitly defined trough the
S^{1}
-valued function

u=\langle\vec{e}_{1},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle-i\langle\vec{e}_{2},\vec{f}_{1}\rangle
,
which belongs to
W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}),S^{1})

and satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle[u]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left([\vec{e}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}+[\vec{f}\,]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}\right)\leq C\varepsilon

for some constant
C>0
.
By means of lemma
A.12
,
we may extend
u
to
S^{1}=\partial B_{1}(0)
, thus obtaining a function

v\in W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0),S^{1})

satisfying
[v]_{W^{1/2,2}(\partial B_{1}(0))}\leq 2[u]_{W^{1/2,2}((-\theta_{0},\theta_{0}))}
.
The rest of the argument is now similar to that of the proof of lemma

A.10
, with
v
in place of
u
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.4
Singular Points of Lipschitz Immersions

Lemma A.14

(
[
60
]
, Lemma A.5)
.

Let
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a measurable map so that,
for every
\delta>0

\Phi:B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
defines a conformal Lipschitz
immersion with
L^{2}
-bounded second fundamental form.
Assume that
\Phi
extends to a map in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and that the Gauss map

\vec{n}_{\Phi}
also extends to a map in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))
.
Then
\Phi
realises a Lipschitz map of the whole disk
B_{1}(0)
and there exists a non-negative
integer
n
and a costant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle(C-o(1))|z|^{n}\leq|\nabla\Phi(z)|\leq(C+o(1))|z|^{n}\quad\text{as }z\to 0.

Lemma A.15
.

Let
\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{M}\}
be points on
\partial B_{1}(0)

and let
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a measurable map so that,
for every
\delta>0
,
\Phi:B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{M}B_{\delta}(b_{i})\to\mathbb{R}^{m}

defines a conformal Lipschitz immersion with
L^{2}
-bounded
second fundamental form, possibly branched at finite number of points

\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\}\subset D
, with
N
independent of
\delta
.
Assume that

1.

\Phi
extends to a map in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
\vec{n}_{\Phi}
extends to a map in
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0),\Gr_{m-2}(\mathbb{R}^{m}))

2.

\log|\nabla\Phi|
extends to a map in
W^{1,p}(B_{1}(0))
for some
p>1
,

3.

\Phi|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\gamma\circ\sigma

and
\vec{n}_{\Phi}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}=\vec{n}_{0}\circ\sigma

for some homeomorphism
\sigma
,

where
\gamma
is an arc-length parametrization of a closed, simple
curve
\Gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
of class
C^{1,1}
and
\vec{n}_{0}
is a unit-normal
m-2
vector field
along
\Gamma
of class
C^{1,1}
.
Then
\Phi
extends to a weak Lipschitz immersion at every point
b_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,M
.

Proof of Lemma
A.15
.

We call
\lambda=\log(|\nabla\Phi|/\sqrt{2})
.
It is enough to prove that, for every
i=1,\ldots,M
,
there exists some
0<s<1
so that

\displaystyle\|\lambda\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{s}(b_{j}))}<+\infty.

(A.35)

Claim 1:
For every
\varepsilon>0
,
the coordinate ortho-normal frame of
\Phi
denoted by

\vec{e}=(\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2})

extends to a map in

W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{N}B_{\varepsilon}(a_{j}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

Proof of claim 1.

We need to prove that
\vec{e}
extends to a a
W^{1,2}
-map
in a neighbourhood of each
b_{i}
.
From the relation
(
A.20
) we have

\displaystyle|\nabla\vec{e}|^{2}=2|\langle\nabla\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}=2|\nabla\lambda|^{2}+|\nabla\vec{n}|^{2}\quad\text{in }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(B_{1}(0)\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{N}\})

consequently, since
\nabla\lambda
belongs to
L^{p}(B_{1}(0))

and
|\nabla\vec{n}|
belongs to
L^{2}(B_{1}(0))
,
we deduce that

|\nabla\vec{e}|
belongs to
L^{p}(B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{M}B_{\varepsilon}(a_{j}))

for every
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence
\vec{e}
belongs to

W^{1,p}(B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{M}B_{\varepsilon}(a_{j}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
the trace of
\vec{e}
on
\partial_{B_{1}(0)}
is well-defined and belongs
to
W^{1/2,1-1/p}(\partial B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
Moreover,
if
\mathbf{t}
is the unit tangent vector of
\Gamma
,
from the boundary conditions this trace is given in
complex notation by

\displaystyle\vec{e}_{1}+i\vec{e}_{2}=\mathrm{e}^{-i\theta}(\star(\mathbf{t}\wedge\vec{n}_{0})+i\mathbf{t})(\sigma)\quad\text{on }\partial B_{1}(0),

so we see that it actually lies in

(W^{1/2,2}\cap C^{0})(\partial B_{1}(0),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
Fix now
i=1,\ldots,M
and choose a sufficiently small
0<r<1

so that no branch point
a_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots M
, lies in
B_{r}(b_{i})\cap D
and
so that, thanks to lemma
A.11
,
we find an ortho-normal Coulomb frame
\vec{g}=(\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2})

belonging to
W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{i}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
which lifts
\vec{n}_{\Phi}
and whose trace on
\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{i})

coincides with that of
\vec{e}
.
If
\varphi
denotes the angle which rotates
\vec{g}
to
\vec{e}
,
from the change of Gauge formula (
A.19
) we
deduce that
\varphi
is harmonic in
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{i})
, moreover

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi|\leq|\langle\nabla\vec{g}_{1},\vec{g}_{2}\rangle|+|\langle\nabla\vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2}\rangle|\quad\text{in. }\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{i})).

Hence
\varphi\in W^{1,p}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{j}))
and thus
has a well-defined trace in
\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r}(b_{j})
which is
is zero by construction.
Hence
\varphi
is smooth on
B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(b_{i})
and so
we deduce that
\vec{e}\in W^{1,2}(B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(b_{j}),\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
Since
i=1,\dots,N
was arbitrary, claim 1 follows.

Claim 2.

\sigma^{\prime}
extends to a map
in
L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))
.

Proof of Claim 2.

From the boundary conditions on
\Phi
we have that, uniformly
on
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{i}B_{\delta}(b_{i})}\sigma^{\prime}=\int_{\partial B_{1}(0)\setminus\cup_{i}B_{\delta}(b_{i})}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma),

hence, since
\sigma
is continuous,
the classical Schwartz lemma for distributions
implies
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}|_{\partial B_{1}(0)}
extends
to a map in
L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))
.
This proves claim 2.

Combining claims 1 and 2, we deduce that
\lambda

is a weak solution of Liouville’s equation
1.15
.
From claim 1,
k_{g}(\sigma)\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}-1
belongs to
L^{1}(\partial B_{1}(0))

hence we may find a sufficiently small
0<r<1
so that, from lemma
2.3

there holds

\|e^{\lambda-\overline{\lambda}}\|_{L^{p}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/2}(b_{i})}

for some
p>1
.
Thanks to claim 2 and possibly reducing
r
so that no branch point
a_{j}
lies
in
D\cap B_{r}(b_{i})
, we can invoke theorem
A.6

and conclude that

\|\lambda-\overline{\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B_{1}(0)\cap B_{r/4}(b_{i}))}
is finite,
which gives the desired estimate
A.35
.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎
